# Hippocampal fields stretch far, and population similarity stays local except in ventral CA3

Very large place fields are real, but a single field rarely covers a large share of the space around it. The absolute record is a **32 m field in dorsal CA1 of an Egyptian fruit bat flying a 200 m tunnel**, about 17% of the 185 m flight path. In that dataset the median field was about 4.8 m, and fields of one neuron differed up to 20-fold in size. Fields covering a third or more of an environment appear in four places: ventral rat CA3 on an 18 m track, where fields averaged 5.52 m, or 31%, few exceeded 10 m, and one drawn field ran about 15.7 m, or 87%; marmoset hippocampus, with a mean of 1.42 m on a 2.44 m track, or 58%; dorsal CA1 in small rooms and short tracks, at 25 to 42%; and the subiculum, whose cells spread 95% of their spikes over 56% of a 1.5 m box against 26% for CA1, through diffuse firing rather than one compact field. When the environment grows, dorsal CA1 fields grow sublinearly and cells add fields instead, so the largest single fields in the 200 m tunnel, an 18.6 m² arena and a 48 m track cover only 17%, 19% and about 7% of the space. **On the second question the answer is a qualified yes, resting on one study, with a block-shaped second case in marmosets.** On an 18 m track, Kjelstrup et al. correlated population vectors at three levels of rat CA3. Each vector pooled cells recorded in different rats and sessions, not a simultaneously recorded ensemble. In ventral CA3 the correlation fell to 0.5 about 3.3 m from a position and then declined smoothly, reaching about zero near 10.5 m, about 64% of the 16.5 m analysed. Intermediate CA3 reached zero near 7 m, while dorsal CA3 fell below 0.1 within about 1 m. No one has replicated the result, and the track ends may truncate ventral fields. Anterior marmoset hippocampus also stays correlated over metres, falling to 0.5 about 1.2 to 1.3 m from a position on a 4.26 m L-shaped track, but its high values form one block per arm rather than a smooth decline, and they come from units pooled over sessions and read from a figure. Every other published curve of population-vector similarity against distance comes from dorsal rodent CA1, CA3, dentate gyrus, subiculum or retrosplenial cortex, posterior and middle marmoset hippocampus, or chickadee hippocampus, in environments no longer than 4.26 m. The rodent and bird curves fall to 0.5 within 5 to 28 cm of a position during normal locomotion, half a field width or less where the same study reports a field width, and most cross zero 20 to 79 cm away. Other hints of broader kernels come only from altered self-motion, an abstract-only comparison of dorsal and middle CA1, and human fMRI of remembered distances. No one has published this curve for dorsal CA1 in an environment longer than a 4 m loop, or for any region in an environment longer than 18 m, although two public datasets, the 48 m track and a 3.5 × 2.5 m arena, would allow it.

Each finding carries a tag that says how it was checked. **[full text]** means the value was read in the paper or its supplement. **[abstract]** means only the abstract was read. **[secondary]** means a later paper, review, commentary, thesis summary or press item reports it. **[unverified]** means it came from a search snippet or aggregator page that could not be checked. **[preprint]** marks work that is not peer reviewed. **[derived]** marks my own arithmetic on reported numbers, mostly conversions into a fraction of the environment. **[figure-read]** means I read the value off a published figure against its axis ticks, so it is approximate. "Figure only" means the paper shows the value in a figure that could not be read numerically.

## Very large fields exist, but few cover much of their environment

The largest single fields come from dorsal CA1 of Egyptian fruit bats flying a 194 m tunnel, 185.3 m between the two landing balls. In 5 wild-born bats, 196 of 235 putative pyramidal cells were place cells, with 1,629 fields in total. **Field size ran from 0.6 to 32 m** and fit a log-normal distribution whose median is about 4.8 m and mean about 5.7 m [full text; median and mean derived] ([Eliav et al. 2021](https://www.weizmann.ac.il/brain-sciences/labs/ulanovsky/sites/brain-sciences.labs.ulanovsky/files/2024-11/Eliav2021.pdf)). The fields of one neuron differed up to 20-fold in size, with a mean largest-to-smallest ratio of 4.4, and the 12.2% of neurons with a single field had fields averaging 5.9 ± 3.5 m [full text] ([Eliav et al. 2021](https://www.weizmann.ac.il/brain-sciences/labs/ulanovsky/sites/brain-sciences.labs.ulanovsky/files/2024-11/Eliav2021.pdf)). The authors bounded each field at 20% of its peak and measured size as the span from the 5th to the 95th percentile of in-field spike positions. The size distribution held across nine detection variants, although a stricter 20th-to-80th percentile rule ended the tail near 20 m instead of 32 m [full text] ([Eliav et al. 2021](https://www.weizmann.ac.il/brain-sciences/labs/ulanovsky/sites/brain-sciences.labs.ulanovsky/files/2024-11/Eliav2021.pdf)). Rats in large environments have smaller fields in absolute terms. On a novel 48 m track, dorsal CA1 fields averaged **65 ± 43 cm**, and the largest reached about 3.3 m, read from a scatter plot [full text, supplement] ([Rich et al. 2014 supplement](https://www.its.caltech.edu/~jkenny/nb250c/papers/Rich-2014-supp.pdf)). In an 18.6 m² arena, dorsal CA1 subfields ranged from 0.023 to 3.46 m², with 78% at 1 m² or less [full text, accepted manuscript] ([Harland et al. 2021](https://par.nsf.gov/servlets/purl/10294326)). Ventral rat CA3 is the one rodent site with fields near 10 m. On an 18 m track, ventral fields averaged 5.52 ± 0.54 m, the authors write that "few fields were longer than 10 m", and the longest field drawn in the paper, from a ventral cell, ran about 15.7 m in one running direction [full text; 15.7 m figure-read] ([Kjelstrup et al. 2008](https://doi.org/10.1126/science.1157086)).

Relative to the environment the ranking reverses. The tables list the largest or typical fields as a fraction of the space, one table per region, each from largest share to smallest with descriptive reports last. The CA1 table gives dorsal or ventral where the source states it.

### Shares fall as spaces grow, and no dorsal field covers a third of a large one

The fraction falls in large spaces because field size grows more slowly than the environment. From their 6 m and 200 m tunnels, Eliav et al. fitted mean field size as rising with tunnel length to the power **0.36** and mean per-cell coverage as falling with length to the power -0.24 [full text] ([Eliav et al. 2021](https://www.weizmann.ac.il/brain-sciences/labs/ulanovsky/sites/brain-sciences.labs.ulanovsky/files/2024-11/Eliav2021.pdf)). In rats, a 5.8-fold larger floor made CA1 fields only 30% larger [full text] ([Fenton et al. 2008](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2695947/)), and the fraction of the arena covered per cell was the same in 8.2 and 18.6 m² arenas, Tukey P = 0.92 [full text] ([Harland et al. 2021](https://par.nsf.gov/servlets/purl/10294326)). Eliav et al. also tested whether small environments hide whole-environment fields among cells that look non-spatial. They argued against it because the share of active cells that were place cells was the same in the 6 m and 200 m tunnels, **83.3% and 83.4%** [full text] ([Eliav et al. 2021](https://www.weizmann.ac.il/brain-sciences/labs/ulanovsky/sites/brain-sciences.labs.ulanovsky/files/2024-11/Eliav2021.pdf)). Detection rules still bias the record against the broadest cells. Tanni et al. rejected candidate fields larger than half the rate map [full text] ([Tanni, de Cothi & Barry 2022](https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC9616721/fullTextXML)), Mankin et al. kept only cells with fields under 25% of the enclosure in at least one session [full text] ([Mankin et al. 2015](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4392894/)), Tingley and Buzsáki capped field width at 120 cm, which is 37.5% of their 3.2 m maze [full text, derived] ([Tingley & Buzsáki 2018](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6605060/)), and spatial-information cutoffs such as 1 bit/spike in macaques remove diffuse cells by construction [full text] ([Mao et al. 2021](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8571030/)).

The answer to the first question is therefore yes in absolute terms and conditional in relative terms. **No source reports a single dorsal field covering a third or more of an environment larger than about 6 m or 2 m².** Fields that large relative to the space are reported only in small arenas, in ventral rodent hippocampus, in pooled marmoset hippocampus, and as diffuse subicular or lateral septal firing that is not a thresholded field. The per-level means for the 18 m track, 0.98 ± 0.03, 3.73 ± 0.43 and 5.52 ± 0.54 m in dorsal, intermediate and ventral CA3, are stated in the paper itself on p. 140. They come from 5, 3 and 4 of the 21 rats recorded, 12 in all, and count as fields "spatially stable contiguous regions with firing above 20% of the peak rate". The ventral fields are bounded by the 18 m track, and the authors note that "fields at the end of the track may be truncated", so the ventral scale in a longer space is unknown [full text; 12 of 21 derived] ([Kjelstrup et al. 2008](https://doi.org/10.1126/science.1157086)). A cell can also cover a large share of an environment through many fields. Bat CA1 cells covered 15% of the tunnel on average, and the coverage histogram reaches roughly 80% for a few cells, read from the figure [full text] ([Eliav et al. 2021](https://www.weizmann.ac.il/brain-sciences/labs/ulanovsky/sites/brain-sciences.labs.ulanovsky/files/2024-11/Eliav2021.pdf)).

## Only ventral rat CA3 shows a kernel that declines smoothly across most of an environment; in anterior marmoset hippocampus similarity forms one block per track arm

Each table lists every measurement found for one region. "Crosses zero" is the first crossing. Curves built from z-scored or peak-scaled rates then dip below zero, and an overlap without mean subtraction never reaches zero, so compare rows that share a measure. Shares are of the length analysed. Rows added from the November 2026 kernel sweep come from sweep_kernel.csv and its audit, audit_sweep_kernel.md, in the literature notes; where the sweep and the audit read a figure differently, the row gives both readings. The retrosplenial rows come from sweep_kernel_rsc.csv and its audit, audit_sweep_rsc.md.

Rubin et al. 2015, listed here in an earlier version, correlates matching positions on two different tracks. That is a between-environment measure, so it now appears only with the remapping results.

Population-vector (PV) similarity has been plotted against the distance between two positions in a limited set of real datasets, all listed in the tables above. Outside intermediate and ventral CA3 and anterior marmoset hippocampus, each rodent or bird curve recorded during normal locomotion falls to 0.5 within 5 to 28 cm of a position in spaces up to about 4 m. The curves then level off, below zero when rates are z-scored and above zero when they are not, except Blair et al.'s cross-half curve, which declines slowly to about +0.05 at 170 cm [full text, figure-read] ([Blair et al. 2023](https://doi.org/10.7554/eLife.80661)). In dorsal CA1 of rats on a 1.8 m track, z-scored PVs built from all 132 CA1 pyramidal cells active in control trials fell to near zero within less than 40 cm, with a spatial scale of about 30 cm, which is **17 to 22% of the track** [full text, derived] ([Green et al. 2022](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC9245695/)). On a 76 cm circular track, Wang et al. show within-session PV cross-correlograms for CA1 and CA3 separately, with bands that narrow at texture boundaries, "especially in CA3", but they measure no band width for either region. Their statement that beyond the average field size "the representations are maximally decorrelated with no further relationship to longer distances" is reasoning in the Discussion, not a measurement. Median field size there was 60° in CA1 and 73° in CA3, about 17 and 20% of the loop [full text, derived] ([Wang, Monaco & Knierim 2020](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7259364/)). Mouse retrosplenial cortex on a 150 cm treadmill belt gave a curve that "steeply falls to zero over a short distance (~35 cm)", in the words of the Discussion. On the Fig. 3d curve it falls to 0.5 at 13.2 cm and crosses zero at 37.8 cm, 25% of the belt. CA1 imaged the same way gives 13.4 and 33 cm [full text, figure-read] ([Mao et al. 2017](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5557927)). A landmark-centred retrosplenial curve in virtual reality falls to 0.5 between 22.5 and 27.9 cm from the landmark, but it correlates two halves of the trials and peaks at only 0.76 to 0.82 [full text, figure-read] ([Fischer et al. 2020](https://doi.org/10.7554/eLife.51458)). Putative dentate granule cells on a 1.76 m track gave 0.5 at 14 to 15 cm in light and 8.4 to 9 cm in darkness [figure-read] ([Gothard et al. 2001](https://doi.org/10.1523/JNEUROSCI.21-18-07284.2001)), and chickadee hippocampus at about 5 cm between cache sites [full text, figure-read] ([Chettih et al. 2024](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.cell.2024.02.032)). In a 150 cm box the subiculum, whose single cells fire over twice the area of CA1 cells, has a PV autocorrelation that falls to 0.5 at 18.6 to 19.2 cm against 22.2 to 22.5 cm in CA1, about 14% narrower. It starts lower, 0.90 against 0.95 at the smallest distance bin, and crosses zero later, at about 60 against 54 cm, so it is narrower only near the top of the curve [full text, figure-read from Fig. S7H] ([Ledergerber et al. 2021](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8170370)). In the subiculum, then, broad single-cell tuning did not produce a broad population kernel, and it did not produce a much narrower one either. Along the CA3 long axis, broader fields did come with a broader kernel, as the next paragraph shows.

The one direct exception comes from CA3 on an 18 m linear track. Kjelstrup et al. stacked the rate maps of all pyramidal cells recorded at each longitudinal level, combining "data from multiple recordings", so each population vector pools cells recorded in different rats and sessions rather than a simultaneously recorded ensemble. They cut the track into 5 cm bins, which gave 330 vectors per running direction once the turning points were removed, and computed the Pearson correlation between every pair of vectors. The band of high correlation was 0.81, 3.71 and 6.66 m wide at r = 0.50, which the paper calls the half-width, and 1.41, 8.65 and 13.59 m wide at r = 0.20, in dorsal, intermediate and ventral CA3 [full text, p. 141] ([Kjelstrup et al. 2008](https://doi.org/10.1126/science.1157086)). The marker lines in the figure span both sides of zero separation, so these are full widths across the diagonal. In ventral CA3 the correlation therefore falls to 0.5 about 3.3 m from a position [derived; 3.4 to 3.6 m figure-read]. Read from the figure, ventral correlation was about 0.70 at 2 m, 0.44 at 4 m, 0.27 at 6 m and 0.15 at 8 m, reached about zero near 10.5 m and stayed between about -0.03 and -0.13 out to 16 m. Intermediate correlation reached about zero near 7 m. Dorsal correlation fell below 0.1 by about 1 m and then stayed between about -0.05 and 0.13 [full text, figure-read] ([Kjelstrup et al. 2008](https://doi.org/10.1126/science.1157086)). The ventral curve declines without a plateau between 1 and 8 m, and its fall to zero spans about 64% of the 16.5 m analysed. At both the intermediate and the ventral level the zero crossing sits at about twice the mean field size [derived]. The authors trust this population width more than single-field sizes, because their detector sometimes split fields and because fields at the track ends may be truncated [full text]. The result has four limits. It comes from one study that no one has replicated. The vectors are a pseudo-population pooled across rats and sessions. The 18 m track cuts off fields that run into its ends, so the track may set part of the ventral width. The main text gives neither the number of cells behind each level's vectors nor any rate normalization.

Kernel width tracks field width where one study measures both. In two studies with fields measured at 20% of peak, rat CA3 at three long-axis levels on an 18 m track and mouse dorsal CA1 on a 1.4 m track, the correlation falls to 0.5 at 0.41 to 0.60 field widths (full width 0.83 to 1.21), against 0.46 for identical Gaussian fields [derived] ([Kjelstrup et al. 2008](https://doi.org/10.1126/science.1157086); [Resnik et al. 2012](https://doi.org/10.1523/JNEUROSCI.6460-11.2012)). Mouse dorsal CA1 on a 1.5 m treadmill belt, with field edges at 20% of peak above baseline, falls to 0.5 at 0.36 of the mean and 0.43 of the median field width, and retrosplenial cortex in the same study at 0.33 and 0.38 [derived] ([Mao et al. 2017](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5557927)). Rat dorsal CA1 and CA3 on a 2 m loop, with fields at 10% of peak, give 0.41 and 0.47, against 0.39 for Gaussian fields at 10% [derived] ([Wang, Monaco & Knierim 2020](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.cub.2020.01.083)). Along the long axis the ratio rises from 0.41 to 0.60, so the kernel grows faster than the measured field [derived]. Part of that rise may come from the ventral fields, which the track ends cut and which the authors say underestimate the spatial scale [full text]. The studies also use different field statistics, the mean of each cell's largest field for Kjelstrup et al. and the median of all fields for the others, and the Gaussian reference holds only when fields are small against the space [derived] (audit_sweep_kernel.md). Two cases depart from the pattern. GluA1 knockout mice had fields about as wide as wild-type mice, 17 against 19 cm, but a kernel half as wide, 0.27 field widths, and the authors call the wild-type code "much more spatially coherent" [full text, figure-read] ([Resnik et al. 2012](https://doi.org/10.1523/JNEUROSCI.6460-11.2012)). So field width alone does not set the kernel. In anterior marmoset hippocampus the kernel is 1.3 to 1.4 field widths, wider than the fields [figure-read, derived] ([Courellis et al. 2019](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6922474/)).

Anterior marmoset hippocampus gives the other broad curve. On a 4.26 m L-shaped track with arms of 2.43 and 1.83 m, the anterior correlation falls to 0.5 about 1.2 to 1.3 m from a position and never crosses zero, with a floor of +0.29 to +0.36. The high values form two blocks, one per arm, and the corner of the L bounds them, so the curve does not decline smoothly along the track. The authors describe "seemingly distinct subcomponents of the place-cell population being sensitive to the long and short arms" [full text]. The middle and posterior implants fall to 0.5 at 56 to 57 and 41 to 42 cm, and both rise again far from the diagonal, posteriorly to about +0.42 near 316 cm, through off-diagonal blocks [full text, figure-read] ([Courellis et al. 2019](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6922474/)). All three levels pool units across sessions and carry 21.5 cm of effective smoothing, which widens them, and the study does not separate CA fields [full text]. The kernel grows from posterior to anterior, as rodent CA3 grows from dorsal to ventral, but no grid spacing has been measured in marmosets, so these curves are not in the heat maps.

The remaining candidate exceptions are weak or indirect. Maurer et al. report that dorsal CA1 PVs "became decorrelated over substantially shorter distances" than PVs in the middle hippocampus, but no distances are available [abstract] ([Maurer et al. 2005](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/16145692/)). A study from the same group measured the distance at which PV overlap drops to 0.5 on a 130 cm diameter ring track and found it **about three times larger** when rats drove a small vehicle than when they walked, about 97 against 27 cm read from the figure. When the rat sat still and the curtained apparatus rotated around it, in other rats on another apparatus, "most cells that were active on the track fired over most of the track" and the overlap never fell below about 0.54 [full text; distances figure-read] ([Terrazas et al. 2005](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6725460)). The claim that ventral PVs "remain correlated over much larger distances" comes from an introduction citing four papers [full text] ([Keinath et al. 2014](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4447627/)). Of these, Kjelstrup et al. measured PV correlation against distance in ventral CA3, as above, and Maurer et al. compared the dorsal pole with the middle hippocampus. Human fMRI shows hippocampal pattern similarity graded with remembered distance from 100 m to 30 km on a log scale [full text] ([Nielson et al. 2015](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4568259)) and across a virtual city about 390 m wide [full text] ([Deuker et al. 2016](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5053807)), but these are voxel patterns during memory retrieval, not the current position. A preprint reanalysed public Neuropixels recordings from Campbell et al. (2021), in which mice ran over a hundred metres in darkness on a virtual-reality treadmill. In two recordings of 44 and 60 distance-tuned cells, grouped ad hoc by peak spacing, the peaks of the population-vector cosine similarity fell over lags of 0 to 8 m, but the trend was "noisy and not truly monotonic" [preprint, v2; values figure-read] ([Dasbehera, Dogra & Redman 2025](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.08292v2)). In bats in a 193 m tunnel with two end compartments (4 bats), PVs for the same place reached after different routes stayed distinguishable for more than 90 m, but that measures history, not spatial distance, and the PVs were smoothed with a 10 m window [full text] ([Maimon et al. 2026](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC13421320)).

The three datasets from larger environments, the 48 m track, the 200 m tunnel and the 18.6 m² arena, contain no such curve. The Rich et al. supplement has 18 figures, none a PV or decoding analysis [full text] ([Rich et al. 2014 supplement](https://www.its.caltech.edu/~jkenny/nb250c/papers/Rich-2014-supp.pdf)). The only population analysis in Eliav et al. ran decoders on synthetic data [full text] ([Eliav et al. 2021](https://www.weizmann.ac.il/brain-sciences/labs/ulanovsky/sites/brain-sciences.labs.ulanovsky/files/2024-11/Eliav2021.pdf)), and Harland et al. inferred that "the population discharges at each location were unique" from field layout without measuring PV similarity [full text] ([Harland et al. 2021, published PDF](https://cenl.ucsd.edu/pubs/Harland-contreras-souder-fellous+CA1-Multiscale-Megaspace+CurrBio+2021.pdf)). The answer to the second question is a qualified yes. **One real-data curve declines gradually across most of an environment under normal locomotion, in ventral rat CA3 on an 18 m track, and intermediate CA3 on the same track declines over about 7 m.** It rests on one unreplicated study, a pseudo-population pooled across rats and sessions, and a track short enough to truncate ventral fields. Anterior marmoset hippocampus stays correlated across most of each arm of a 4.26 m track, but as one block per arm rather than a gradual decline. Every dorsal rodent curve is local, including dorsal CA3 on the same track. The test has not been run on dorsal CA1 in environments longer than a 4 m loop, or on any region in an environment longer than 18 m. Two cautions apply to anyone who runs it. The far-distance floor depends on how PVs are built. Per-cell z-scoring pulls the mean correlation toward zero, while unnormalized rates that include silent cells leave a positive floor. Multi-field cells in large spaces could also raise the far-distance floor without creating a slope, since field positions of different cells on the 48 m track were uncorrelated at scales of 1 to 16 m [full text] ([Rich et al. 2014](https://www.its.caltech.edu/~jkenny/nb250c/papers/rich_14.pdf)). Both points are my reading of the methods, not reported results.

## Thirteen measures at a glance

| Measure | What is measured | Typical values and ranges | Key sources | How firm |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Field size, absolute | Thresholded field length (1D) or area (2D) | Rodent dorsal CA1 24 to 75 cm on 0.8 to 2.5 m tracks, about 0.13 to 0.27 m² in 1 to 2 m boxes; rat 48 m track mean 0.65 m, max about 3.3 m; rat 18.6 m² arena 0.02 to 3.5 m²; bat 200 m tunnel 0.6 to 32 m, median about 4.8 m | Mizuseki 2012; Ziv 2013; Rich 2014; Harland 2021; Eliav 2021 | Full text |
| Field size, fraction of environment | Field extent over environment extent | 25 to 42% in small arenas and short tracks; 17 to 19% at most in 18.6 m² and 200 m; 7% on 48 m; ventral CA3 mean 31% of 18 m, one drawn field about 87%; marmoset 58% of 2.44 m | Fenton 2008; Diba 2008; Eliav 2021; Harland 2021; Kjelstrup 2008; Courellis 2019 | Full text; Kjelstrup example field figure-read |
| Fields per cell and scaling | Count per cell against environment size | About 1 to 1.3 below 1 m²; 2.3 at 8.75 m²; 2 to 5 in 82% of cells at 18.6 m²; mean about 3.5 per 48 m, overdispersed; bat 4.9 per direction over 185 m; field size grows as length to the 0.36. Counting every cell, 0.029 to 0.036 fields per smallest rat grid period on the 48 m track and 0.025 to 0.030 per mouse grid period on a 41 m virtual path; counts from single small spaces run higher | Tanni 2022; Harland 2021; Rich 2014; Eliav 2021; Fenton 2008; Park 2011; Lee 2020 | Full text; Lee 2020 preprint |
| Dorsoventral gradient | Field scale and place-cell share along the long axis | Rat CA3 on an 18 m track, dorsal to ventral, mean field 0.98, 3.73 and 5.52 m and PV band full width at r = 0.5 of 0.81, 3.71 and 6.66 m; fewer ventral cells with fields; ventral fields 39% larger on radial arms; mouse ventral CA1 broader; decoding equal or coarser | Kjelstrup 2008; Royer 2010; Keinath 2014; Jung 1994; Chockanathan 2021 | Mixed: Maurer abstract; Kjelstrup, Royer, Keinath, Chockanathan, Jung full text |
| Subregions | Same-animal comparisons | Open-field median CA1 1,775 cm², CA3 1,275 cm²; CA2 fields 24.5% larger than CA1; granule cells about 1 field, mossy cells about 2.2; bat CA3 74% single-field against CA1 30% | Mizuseki 2012; Mankin 2015; GoodSmith 2017; Senzai 2017; Maimon 2026 | Full text |
| Species | Place-cell share, field size | Bat CA1 83% place cells; marmoset 14% of units; macaque 26%, falling to 7% after modelling facing location; titmouse 60%, zebra finch 27%; barn owl 27% | Eliav 2021; Courellis 2019; Mao 2021; Payne 2021; Agarwal 2023 | Full text |
| Subiculum | Rate, information, coverage, cell types, population code | Rate 2 to 7 times CA1; bits/spike 3 to 8 times lower; 95% of spikes in 56% of bins against 26%; any firing in 84% of a 76 cm cylinder; activity fraction 0.31 proximal to 0.69 distal; boundary vector cells 10 to 36%; decodes at least as well as CA1 at 100 or more cells; correlation dimension 5.2 to 5.8 against 3.0 to 3.6 | Kim 2012; Ledergerber 2021; Kitanishi 2021; Lee 2018; Nakai 2024; Muessig 2024; Sharp & Green 1994 | Full text; Sharp 1997, 1999 and 2006 abstract |
| Active fraction, silent cells | Share of principal cells with fields per environment | CA1 20 to 50%, about two thirds silent; CA3 18 to 30%; granule cells 2 to 10%; mossy cells 88%; 65% of CA1 recruited on 48 m; over 80% in bat 200 m tunnel | Thompson & Best 1989; Rich 2014; GoodSmith 2017; Ziv 2013; Maimon 2026 | Mixed |
| PV similarity against distance | Correlation of PVs at two positions in one environment | Dorsal rodent CA1, CA3, dentate gyrus and subiculum fall to 0.5 within 8 to 27 cm in spaces up to about 4 m, and chickadee hippocampus within about 5 cm; dorsal CA3 on an 18 m track falls below 0.1 by about 1 m; on that track, in one unreplicated study with PVs pooled across rats and sessions, ventral CA3 falls to 0.5 about 3.3 m from a position and to zero near 10.5 m, 64% of the 16.5 m analysed, and intermediate CA3 to zero near 7 m; anterior marmoset hippocampus falls to 0.5 about 1.2 to 1.3 m away on a 4.26 m track, as one block per arm; RSC to 0.5 at 13 cm and to zero at 35 to 38 cm; subiculum 14% narrower than CA1 at r = 0.5 but crossing zero later; full widths at r = 0.5 are 0.34 to 1.4 smallest grid spacings up to about 4 m and 1.8 to 2.5 ventral MEC spacings in ventral CA3; no dorsal CA1 curve for spaces above a 4 m loop | Green 2022; Wang 2020; Gothard 2001; Resnik 2012; Mao 2017; Ledergerber 2021; Kjelstrup 2008; Courellis 2019; Chettih 2024 | Full text, widths mostly figure-read; Maurer abstract |
| Remapping, orthogonality | Similarity between environments | CA3 active-set overlap between rooms 0.11 to 0.14, equal to chance; CA1 0.55 for identical boxes against 0.63 to 0.66 for repeats; 11 orthogonal CA3 maps; VR CA1 PV correlation 0.10 | Leutgeb 2004; Alme 2014; GoodSmith 2017; Gauthier & Tank 2018 | Full text, except Alme (abstract) |
| Decoding accuracy | Position error from ensembles | Median or RMS 5 to 12 cm in 0.5 to 10.3 m environments with 23 to 500 cells; under 1% of a 10.3 m track; CA1 saturates at about 350 to 1,400 cells near 10 cm | Davidson 2009; Brown 1998; Ziv 2013; Hazon 2022; Tampuu 2019 | Full text, Brown abstract |
| Dimensionality | Intrinsic and linear dimension of population activity | About 1 per running direction on a track; CA1 3.0 to 3.6; subiculum 5.2 to 5.8; VR task 4 to 6; 29 to 47 linear PCs for a 4 to 6 dimensional manifold | Rubin 2019; Nakai 2024; Nieh 2021 | Full text |
| Field width over grid spacing | A place field's width divided by one grid module's spacing | Rat 0.094 to 1.9 in tracks up to 3 m and arenas up to 4 m², and up to 4.5 in arenas of 4.38 and 8.75 m². On the 18 m track, CA3 against box-measured modules is 0.57 to 2.9 dorsal, 1.8 to 9.6 intermediate and 2.4 to 14 ventral, and against the same-named MEC level on the same track 0.57 to 1.2, 1.0 to 1.8 and 1.1 to 2.1. Mouse 0.19 to 1.4 in real environments up to 2 m and 0.41 to 2.2 on virtual tracks. Mouse retrosplenial fields 0.33 to 1.6; one dorsal non-grid MEC field 1.55 grid spacings measured in the same mice, and border width 0.40 | Stensola 2012; Brun 2008; Kjelstrup 2008; Tanni 2022; Mizuseki 2012; Komorowski 2013; Zong 2022; Wen 2024; Hainmueller 2018; Navratilova 2026; Mao 2017; Mallory 2018 | Mixed |

## Field size runs from 25 cm in lab arenas to 32 m in a bat tunnel

In standard laboratory spaces, dorsal CA1 fields are a few tens of centimetres across, and CA3 fields are smaller. The largest same-animal comparison recorded 3,670 CA1 cells in 13 rats and 2,203 CA3 cells in 6 of them. Median open-field fields were **1,775 cm² in CA1 and 1,275 cm² in CA3**, equal-area circles of 47.5 and 40.3 cm, and 5.5 and 3.9% of a 180 × 180 cm box. On a 250 cm track the values were 75 cm and 50 cm, on 75 cm radial arms 38.3 and 33.8 cm, and on a zigzag maze 48 and 34 cm [full text; diameters and fractions derived] ([Mizuseki et al. 2012](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3718552/)). In a 200 cm cylinder, proximal, intermediate and distal CA1 fields averaged 2,208, 2,688 and 1,931 cm², 6 to 9% of the floor [full text, derived] ([Henriksen et al. 2010](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3093538/)). Mouse CA1 fields imaged on an 84 cm track had a median width of 24 cm [full text] ([Ziv et al. 2013](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3784308)), and two-photon fields on a 180 cm virtual track averaged 50 ± 19 cm [full text] ([Dombeck et al. 2010](https://doi.org/10.1038/nn.2648)). Rat CA1 fields on a T maze averaged 29.1 ± 14.7 cm [full text] ([Wirtshafter & Wilson 2020](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7274787/)). A narrow definition gives smaller numbers. Counting only bins at least 1.5 SD above a cell's mean rate, mouse dorsal CA1 fields on a 1.9 m virtual track were 9.4 ± 2.0 cm per session [full text] ([Chockanathan & Padmanabhan 2021](https://www.eneuro.org/content/8/5/ENEURO.0211-21.2021)).

One-dimensional and two-dimensional sizes are not interchangeable, even in one lab. Mizuseki's CA1 open-field median corresponds to a 47.5 cm circle, yet the same region's track fields were 75 cm [derived] ([Mizuseki et al. 2012](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3718552/)). Track fields are also counted per running direction, while open-field fields are counted per cell.

In large spaces, absolute size depends on species and on position within the space. The bat fields of 0.6 to 32 m, the rat fields averaging 0.65 m on the 48 m track and the subfields of up to 3.46 m² in the 18.6 m² arena are described above. Bat field peak rate rose with field size, ρ = 0.29 over 1,629 fields, with mean in-field and out-of-field rates of 5.01 and 0.24 Hz [full text] ([Eliav et al. 2021](https://www.weizmann.ac.il/brain-sciences/labs/ulanovsky/sites/brain-sciences.labs.ulanovsky/files/2024-11/Eliav2021.pdf)). On the 48 m track, field size did not correlate with location along the track or with how many other fields the cell had, ρ = 0.05 for both [full text] ([Rich et al. 2014 supplement](https://www.its.caltech.edu/~jkenny/nb250c/papers/Rich-2014-supp.pdf)). In the 18.6 m² arena, cells with more subfields had smaller subfields, but each cell's summed area did not depend on subfield count, r = 0.027 [full text] ([Harland et al. 2021](https://par.nsf.gov/servlets/purl/10294326)). Position matters in two dimensions. Fields grew with distance from the nearest wall in arenas up to 8.75 m², H = 10.5, p = 0.015 in the largest, mostly through width perpendicular to the wall, while field density was highest near walls [full text] ([Tanni, de Cothi & Barry 2022](https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC9616721/fullTextXML)). Subfield size correlated with distance to the nearest wall at r = 0.36 in the 18.6 m² arena [full text] ([Harland et al. 2021](https://par.nsf.gov/servlets/purl/10294326)). In the bat tunnel, fields within 4 m of the food-baited landing balls were smaller and denser, with size rising with distance from the ball at ρ = 0.64, while field size bore no relation to the 13 landmarks [full text] ([Eliav et al. 2021](https://www.weizmann.ac.il/brain-sciences/labs/ulanovsky/sites/brain-sciences.labs.ulanovsky/files/2024-11/Eliav2021.pdf)).

### Field definitions decide the largest field a study can report

Field definitions differ several-fold, and some cap size outright. The table gives each study's rule as reported [full text for each row].

| Study | Edge threshold | Minimum size | Upper cap or exclusion | Bins and smoothing |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Mizuseki 2012 | 10% of peak, peak above 2 Hz, coherence above 0.7 | 15 cm on track, 225 cm² in open field | None stated | 5 cm bins, Gaussian SD 5 cm on track, adaptive in open field |
| Henriksen 2010 | 20% of peak, peak at least 0.25 Hz | 225 cm² | None stated | 5 cm, adaptive |
| Keinath 2014 | 20% of peak | 80 cm² | None stated | 70 × 70 grid, Gaussian SD 3 cm |
| Royer 2010 | 20% of peak | None stated | None stated | 200 × 200 pixel maps, Gaussian half-width 5 pixels, 15 for ventral zigzag cells |
| Fenton 2008 | Any rate above 0 | 9 contiguous 4.7 cm pixels, about 200 cm² | None | None described |
| Park 2011 | Any rate above 0 | 9 contiguous 2.8 cm pixels, about 71 cm² | None | Not obtained |
| Rich 2014 | 2 Hz, spikes on at least half of passes | 15 cm | None | 1 cm bins, Gaussian SD 10 cm |
| Eliav 2021 | 20% of field peak, peaks above 1 Hz, sub-peaks merged if the dip stays above 50% | Spikes on at least 5 laps or 20% of laps | Takeoff and landing zones excluded | 20 cm bins, Gaussian σ 0.5 m |
| Harland 2021 | 1.2 SD above the mean of all bins, per cell | 200 cm² | None | 12 × 12 camera pixels, Hanning |
| Tanni 2022 | Iterative from 1 Hz in 0.05 Hz steps, peak at least 2 Hz | 10 bins | Fields larger than half the map rejected | 4 cm, Gaussian SD 2 bins |
| Mankin 2015 | In supplement | In supplement | Cells kept only with fields under 25% of the enclosure in one session or more | In supplement |
| Tingley & Buzsáki 2018 | 20% of peak, peak at least 2 Hz | 8 cm | 120 cm | About 1.5 cm |
| Courellis 2019 | 20% of peak | None stated | None stated | 100 bins, 5-point Gaussian |
| Wirtshafter & Wilson 2020 | Peak above mean + 2 SD, edge at mean + 1 SD | 15 cm | None stated | 2 cm, Gaussian SD 10 cm |
| Diehl 2017 | 0.3 of peak, peak at least 1 Hz | 250 cm² | None stated | 5 cm, Gaussian SD about 1 bin |
| Kjelstrup 2008 | 20% of peak, spatially stable and contiguous | None stated | None; fields at the track ends may be truncated | Not given in the main text |
| Muller & Kubie 1987 | Every pixel above 1.0 AP/s | 9 contiguous pixels | None | Pixel size not given in this paper |
| O'Keefe & Burgess 1996 | Maps shown only with a peak above 1.0 Hz, displayed in steps of 20% of peak | None stated | None | Bins 1/24 of the box area, so they grow with the box |

The effects are measurable where authors tested them. A fixed 1 Hz threshold left 45 of 383 cells with no field, where the cell-specific threshold found fields in all of them [full text] ([Harland et al. 2021](https://par.nsf.gov/servlets/purl/10294326)). Royer et al. smoothed ventral zigzag maps with a kernel three times wider than dorsal maps, which can enlarge measured ventral fields [full text] ([Royer et al. 2010](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2825159/)). Kjelstrup et al. write that their detector "sometimes cleaves fields as a result of local rate variations" [full text]. The longest ventral field drawn in that paper, about 15.7 m in one running direction, was split into two fields of about 2.4 and 9.6 m in the other [full text, figure-read] ([Kjelstrup et al. 2008](https://doi.org/10.1126/science.1157086)). Fenton and Park count any contiguous nonzero firing, which includes the low-rate skirt of a field and can merge neighbours [full text] ([Fenton et al. 2008](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2695947/); [Park, Dvorak & Fenton 2011](https://journals.plos.org/plosone/article?id=10.1371/journal.pone.0022349)). Fenton et al. report fields as 0.27 of the 68 cm cylinder, but 57.8 pixels of 22 cm² is about 1,280 cm², or 0.35 of the floor, so the paper's denominator or averaging differs from the obvious one [full text; discrepancy derived] ([Fenton et al. 2008](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2695947/)).

**Full citations.** Mizuseki K, Royer S, Diba K, Buzsáki G (2012). Activity dynamics and behavioral correlates of CA3 and CA1 hippocampal pyramidal neurons. ## Larger spaces add fields faster than they enlarge them

Every study that enlarged an environment found more fields per cell, and most found larger fields, but field size grew far more slowly than the space. The classic rescaling experiments showed mixed behaviour within one population. When a 76 cm cylinder was doubled in diameter and height, which quadruples floor area, **36% of 25 cells recorded in both kept a field at the same angular and relative radial position, 52% had a field in only one cylinder or unrelated fields, and 12% were nearly silent in both**, counts of 9, 13 and 3. The authors had selected cells with a clear field in the small cylinder or small rectangle. Field area did not quadruple. For the 9 scaling cells, the ratio of large to small field size averaged 0.87 and ranged from 0.24 to 2.06. It rose to 1.73 when the authors equalized dwell time per unit area and to 2.49 when they merged 2 × 2 pixels, and they concluded that "sampling problems may be obscuring an interesting relationship between field sizes". Doubling a 40 × 56 cm rectangle gave scaled fields in 7 of 19 cells [full text] ([Muller & Kubie 1987](https://doi.org/10.1523/JNEUROSCI.07-07-01951.1987)). The companion paper notes that "a single cell may have more than one field in a given apparatus" [abstract] ([Muller, Kubie & Ranck 1987](https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=EXT_ID:3612225&resultType=core&format=json)). O'Keefe and Burgess recorded 28 fields from 27 CA1 and CA3 complex-spike cells in 6 rats in four boxes, 61 × 61, 61 × 122, 122 × 61 and 122 × 122 cm, with 61 cm walls. In 38 of 56 field-by-axis cases the peak sat at a fixed distance from one wall or a fixed proportion of the distance between two, and 28 of those 38 were distances [full text; split derived from Table 1b]. Peaks in the illustrated cells sat 2.7 to 36.5 cm from the walls that fixed them. Of 112 rate maps in Table 1a, 16 had no field, 69 had one compact peak, 11 had one stretched peak, 13 had two peaks and 3 had more, and 9 of 28 fields showed a second peak in at least one larger box. In 22 of 24 stretched or double-peaked maps, from 14 of 15 cells, the peak moved with running direction. The paper reports no field areas, and its bins were 1/24 of each box's area, so bin size grew with the box [full text] ([O'Keefe & Burgess 1996](https://doi.org/10.1038/381425a0)). Shortening a linear track from 170 to 100 cm in 3 rats kept 1,147 of 1,750 CA1 and CA3 fields, which shrank from **52 to 42 cm**, a 19% drop for a 41% shorter track, p < 10⁻¹² [full text] ([Diba & Buzsáki 2008](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2656938/)).

Open arenas of 2 to 19 m² show the same pattern with larger numbers. In 4 rats, dorsal CA1 cells averaged 1.3 ± 0.03 fields in a 68 cm cylinder and 3.4 ± 0.11 on the floor of a 150 × 140 cm chamber, or 4.05 counting its stairs. Single-field place cells fell from 72% to 11%, fields grew by about 30%, and the per-field share of the floor fell from 0.27 to 0.073. Multiple fields were irregular, and only 7.6% of chamber cells had autocorrelogram angles in the 50 to 70° range of a hexagonal grid [full text] ([Fenton et al. 2008](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2695947/)). In a 1.8 × 1.4 m box, 5.6 times the area of a 76 cm cylinder, fields per cell were **5.89 in CA1, 4.96 in CA3 and 5.35 in DG**, and single-field cells fell from 60.7 to 17.9% in CA1, 81.8 to 29% in CA3 and 63.9 to 8.2% in DG. The largest field per cell grew, but pooled field size did not, F(1,1316) = 0.01, p = 0.93. CA3 and DG each came from one rat [full text] ([Park, Dvorak & Fenton 2011](https://journals.plos.org/plosone/article?id=10.1371/journal.pone.0022349)). Across four arenas from 1.09 to 8.75 m² in 5 rats with 629 CA1 place cells, fields per cell rose from **1.28 to 2.28**, field count rose linearly with area, r = 0.983, as did summed field area, r = 0.996, and 84.8% of cells had more than one field across the four arenas. A gamma-Poisson distribution fit field counts better than a Poisson distribution with equal rates, and its extrapolation puts 99% of place cells at one or more fields above 51.8 m² [full text] ([Tanni, de Cothi & Barry 2022](https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC9616721/fullTextXML)). Across arenas of 0.54, 2.16, 8.2 and 18.6 m², 94% of cells had a single field in the smallest arena, 91% had one or two subfields at 2.16 m², and **82% had two to five subfields** at 18.6 m². Subfield count rose linearly with area, R² = 0.978, and summed area per cell was 1.99 to 2.15 m², or 10.7 to 11.6% of the largest arena [full text, fractions derived] ([Harland et al. 2021](https://par.nsf.gov/servlets/purl/10294326)).

The 48 m track and the 200 m tunnel give the cleanest statistics because both are long and one-dimensional. On the track, extended in stages to 3, 10, 22 and 48 m and analysed only on novel sections, 253 putative pyramidal cells from 5 rats averaged 2.29 to 4.33 fields per rat. Field counts were overdispersed and fit a negative binomial with r = 0.57 and p = 0.14, which equals a Poisson process per cell whose rate is gamma distributed with shape 0.57 and rate 7.75, in fields per metre. Some cells had close to 30 fields. For the 61 cells with six or more fields, field positions were uniform and gaps exponential, with 0 of 61 deviating from a Poisson process, and positions of different cells were uncorrelated at bins of 1 to 16 m. Field density did not vary with track angle, distance to corners or running speed, and slow-wave-sleep firing before exposure predicted later field count, r = 0.45 [full text] ([Rich et al. 2014](https://www.its.caltech.edu/~jkenny/nb250c/papers/rich_14.pdf); [Rich et al. 2014 supplement](https://www.its.caltech.edu/~jkenny/nb250c/papers/Rich-2014-supp.pdf)). In the bat tunnel, cells averaged **4.9 fields per flight direction**, some more than 10, with uniform positions, exponential gaps and no spatial periodicity in any bat. A 6 m section of the same tunnel gave one or two fields per cell, and a 130 m section gave intermediate values. The multiscale code was present on the first day of exposure, with no trend over 30 days, and in 3 lab-born bats raised in a 5.3 × 5.0 m room [full text] ([Eliav et al. 2021](https://www.weizmann.ac.il/brain-sciences/labs/ulanovsky/sites/brain-sciences.labs.ulanovsky/files/2024-11/Eliav2021.pdf)). A later study recorded CA1 and CA3 in 15 bats in tunnels up to 200 m. In tunnels of 130 to 200 m, **29.6% of CA1 cell-directions had one field against 73.7% in CA3**, and five or more fields occurred in 31.9% of CA1 place cells against 0.7% of CA3 place cells. CA1 field number rose with tunnel length, CA3 field number stayed low, and field size grew in both [full text] ([Maimon et al. 2026](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC13421320)). In mice imaged across several large virtual environments, each cell's field propensity varied about 10-fold between neighbouring cells and was preserved across environments and months [abstract] ([Lee et al. 2020](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/33035454/)).

Putting these on a common scale needs arithmetic that the papers do not do. The rat gamma fit implies a mean of 0.57 / 7.75 = 0.074 fields per metre across all cells, silent ones included, while 4.9 bat fields over about 185 m is 0.026 fields per metre among place cells only [derived]. The spread of field sizes relative to the mean is similar in the two datasets, a coefficient of variation of 0.66 for the rats and about 0.63 implied by the bat log-normal [derived]. The bat value is approximate, because the log field sizes in that dataset are skewed, about -0.72 where a log-normal gives 0 [full text, figure-read] ([Mainali, Azeredo da Silveira & Burak 2025](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.neuron.2025.01.017)). Per-cell coverage was 15% of the tunnel in bats [full text] ([Eliav et al. 2021](https://www.weizmann.ac.il/brain-sciences/labs/ulanovsky/sites/brain-sciences.labs.ulanovsky/files/2024-11/Eliav2021.pdf)), about 11% of the 18.6 m² arena [full text, derived] ([Harland et al. 2021](https://par.nsf.gov/servlets/purl/10294326)), and about 5% of the 48 m track, from 3.5 fields of 0.65 m [derived]. Implied growth exponents are 0.36 for bat field length against tunnel length, about 0.15 for rat field area against floor area from Fenton's 30% for 5.8 times the area, and about 0.4 for field length against track length from Diba and Buzsáki [derived]. Muller and Kubie's fourfold larger cylinder gives field-area exponents of -0.10 from the raw ratio, 0.40 with dwell time equalized and 0.66 with merged pixels [derived].

The studies disagree on several points. Fenton found pooled fields 30% larger in a bigger room while Park found no change in pooled size after a similar enlargement, and the zero-rate field definition both labs used may fragment or merge fields differently as rooms grow [inference] ([Fenton et al. 2008](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2695947/); [Park, Dvorak & Fenton 2011](https://journals.plos.org/plosone/article?id=10.1371/journal.pone.0022349)). Fenton and Park's 3.4 to 5.9 fields per cell in boxes of about 2 m² exceed Tanni's 2.28 in 8.75 m², again most likely because of thresholds [inference]. Harland et al. argue that the 48 m track's novelty, single dimension and smaller floor may hide multi-scale structure in rats, and Rich et al. did not report within-cell size ratios, so whether rat cells are multiscale in the bat sense on a long track is untested [full text] ([Harland et al. 2021](https://par.nsf.gov/servlets/purl/10294326)). Eliav et al. write that rodent field counts were "much smaller" than theirs, which holds against the 1D rodent literature but not against the 2D box counts [full text] ([Eliav et al. 2021](https://www.weizmann.ac.il/brain-sciences/labs/ulanovsky/sites/brain-sciences.labs.ulanovsky/files/2024-11/Eliav2021.pdf)). Eliav et al. also note that the 48 m track was a zigzag of roughly 1 m segments and that the 18 m track of Kjelstrup et al. passed through several small rooms, so before their study no place-cell recording had used a single open compartment longer than about 10 m [full text] ([Eliav et al. 2021](https://www.weizmann.ac.il/brain-sciences/labs/ulanovsky/sites/brain-sciences.labs.ulanovsky/files/2024-11/Eliav2021.pdf)). Familiarity and speed matter less than size. Megaspace maps were less stable between two visits than small-arena maps, t(920) = 8.33, and speed-matched sessions removed a small difference in summed subfield area between robot-following and foraging rats [full text] ([Harland et al. 2021](https://par.nsf.gov/servlets/purl/10294326)). In bats, the within-cell field-size ratio did not track the speed ratio at those fields, ρ = 0.03 [full text] ([Eliav et al. 2021](https://www.weizmann.ac.il/brain-sciences/labs/ulanovsky/sites/brain-sciences.labs.ulanovsky/files/2024-11/Eliav2021.pdf)).

## Field scale rises five- to eightfold toward the ventral pole, in one study

The dorsoventral gradient rests on one study. Kjelstrup et al. recorded CA3 in 21 rats running back and forth on an 18 m linear track and grouped recording sites by position along the long axis, dorsal at 7 to 22%, intermediate at 40 to 70% and ventral at 70 to 100%. The supplement adds that the rats were male Long-Evans recorded with tetrodes on a straight track that crossed three rooms, that the track cells sat at 7 to 19%, 55 to 70% and 71 to 87% of the CA3 axis, and that ventral largest fields reached 15.9 m of the 16.5 m analysed, so the track caps some of them [full text; positions and widths figure-read] ([Kjelstrup et al. 2008 supplement](https://www.science.org/doi/suppl/10.1126/science.1157086)). Fields were "spatially stable contiguous regions with firing above 20% of the peak rate". Mean field size, ± SEM, was 0.98 ± 0.03 m for 111 dorsal cells from 5 rats, 3.73 ± 0.43 m for 37 intermediate cells from 3 rats and 5.52 ± 0.54 m for 61 ventral cells from 4 rats, or 5, 21 and 31% of the track [full text; fractions derived]. The legend of Fig. 4 calls this measure "the average width of the largest place field of individual cells", so the means may describe each cell's largest field. "Few fields were longer than 10 m", and one ventral cell drawn in the paper had a single field from about 1.2 to 16.9 m in one running direction, 87% of the track [full text; length figure-read]. The central peak of each cell's spatial autocorrelation, measured at 20% of its height, was 1.12, 3.07 and 4.10 m wide at the three levels. Theta phase precession appeared at every level. Its slope fell from -102° ± 33° per m dorsally to -18° ± 9° per m in intermediate and ventral cells combined, and in ventral CA3 "the phase advance often occurred over distances as large as 10 m". The authors read this as showing that ventral firing regions "are part of one place field rather than multiple overlapping fields" [full text] ([Kjelstrup et al. 2008](https://doi.org/10.1126/science.1157086)).

The population estimate scales the same way. The PV band was 0.81, 3.71 and 6.66 m wide at r = 0.5 across the diagonal, and in ventral CA3 the correlation reached zero only about 10.5 m from a position, as described in the second section [full text; zero crossing figure-read]. The authors trust this population width more than single-field sizes, because their detector "occasionally divided fields as a consequence of local rate variations" and because "fields at the end of the track may be truncated". Their summary is that the map "expands approximately linearly from a scale of <1 m near the dorsal pole to ~10 m near the ventral pole" [full text] ([Kjelstrup et al. 2008](https://doi.org/10.1126/science.1157086)). Left-run and right-run fields were more correlated in ventral than in dorsal CA3, yet for discrete environments ventral CA3 "still had strongly orthogonalized place maps" [full text, note 33]. In the supplement, left-run and right-run maps correlated at r = 0.142, 0.394 and 0.505 from dorsal to ventral. Rate overlap between two rooms was 0.12 ± 0.06 in dorsal CA3 against 0.18 expected by chance, and 0.22 ± 0.07 and 0.27 ± 0.04 at the intermediate and ventral levels against 0.21, none different from chance, while repeated recordings in one room gave 0.57, 0.75 and 0.68 [full text] ([Kjelstrup et al. 2008 supplement](https://www.science.org/doi/suppl/10.1126/science.1157086)). A same-year commentary describes ventral neurons that "fire almost everywhere in an environment in one room, and nowhere in an identical environment in another room" [secondary] ([Hasselmo 2008](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2590634)). The main text does not describe the track's layout, which Eliav et al. say passed through several small rooms [full text] ([Eliav et al. 2021](https://www.weizmann.ac.il/brain-sciences/labs/ulanovsky/sites/brain-sciences.labs.ulanovsky/files/2024-11/Eliav2021.pdf)). A later paper reports that fewer than 10% of ventral CA3 cells in that study showed reliable phase precession [secondary] ([Royer et al. 2010](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2825159/)), a share the Kjelstrup main text does not give. Because the track was only 18 m long, a 10 m ventral field cannot be independent of the track's ends, so the ventral scale in a longer open space is unknown [inference].

Other rat studies agree that ventral cells fire more broadly but disagree on how spatial they are. In a familiar 68 × 68 cm box, 18% of "ventral" CA1 complex-spike cells had place fields against 45% of dorsal ones, 22 of 129 against 36 of 80. Ventral fields covered 272 against 67 bins of 6.9 cm², equal-area diameters of about 49 and 24 cm, and "some ventral place fields were as large as 80% of the entire recording apparatus". The authors place their sites at about 20% and 65% of the septotemporal axis [full text; diameters derived] ([Jung, Wiener & McNaughton 1994](https://doi.org/10.1523/JNEUROSCI.14-12-07347.1994)). In a cylinder, ventral cells had positional firing "very similar" to dorsal place cells [abstract] ([Poucet, Thinus-Blanc & Muller 1994](https://doi.org/10.1097/00001756-199410270-00014)). Kjelstrup et al. point out that this study did not verify recording sites histologically and found fields of dorsal size, so "it is hard to compare the results" [full text, note 29] ([Kjelstrup et al. 2008](https://doi.org/10.1126/science.1157086)). Recording CA1 at the extreme dorsal pole and in the middle hippocampus, Maurer et al. found smaller fields and higher rates dorsally and a falling probability of a field toward the temporal pole [abstract] ([Maurer et al. 2005](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/16145692/)). Kjelstrup et al. place the intermediate recordings of Maurer et al. and of Jung et al. at about 30 to 45% of the dorsal-to-ventral axis [secondary, Kjelstrup note 29] ([Kjelstrup et al. 2008](https://doi.org/10.1126/science.1157086)), where Jung et al.'s own estimate is about 65% [full text]. Royer et al. recorded 1,546 dorsal and 351 ventral CA3 pyramidal cells in 6 rats in a 1 × 2 m open field, a seven-arm radial maze and a zigzag maze. Ventral cells "rarely showed continuous two-dimensional place fields", most "lacked the defining criteria of place cells", single-arm fields were **39% larger** in ventral CA3, p < 0.01, and ventral cells typically fired on several arms. Left and right journey field sizes correlated at r = 0.81 in ventral CA3 against -0.02 dorsally. Absolute sizes are figure only [full text] ([Royer et al. 2010](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2825159/)). In a context task with two 37 × 37 cm boxes, ventral CA3 fields "usually extended only to areas adjacent to the location of one object", and after overtraining, ventral ensembles of 2 to 5 cells generalized across locations within a context more than dorsal ensembles did [full text] ([Komorowski et al. 2013](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3667351)).

Mouse data show smaller differences and conflict on precision. In 35 and 70 cm cylinders, ventral CA1 fields were larger than dorsal, F(1,197) = 5.9, and grew in the larger cylinder, P < 0.001. The authors describe ventral cells as "broadly tuned" and say they "frequently span the entirety of the environment". Field count per cell did not differ by region, and population location reconstruction showed no region effect, F(1,70) = 2.1, with 89 dorsal cells from 6 mice and 65 ventral cells from 5 mice [full text] ([Keinath et al. 2014](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4447627/)). On a 1.9 m virtual track, head-fixed mice gave ventral CA1 fields of 11.9 ± 6.3 cm against 9.4 ± 2.0 cm dorsally per session, not significantly different, but ventral maps were "coarser and less precise" with lower spatial information, and dorsal activity had higher linear dimensionality [full text] ([Chockanathan & Padmanabhan 2021](https://www.eneuro.org/content/8/5/ENEURO.0211-21.2021)). The ventral site in that study, 4.25 mm deep, may be intermediate rather than the ventral pole [inference].

Long-axis gradients appear in other species too, though homology is uncertain. In marmosets, field width "significantly decreased the further posterior the recording was conducted" [full text] ([Courellis et al. 2019](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6922474/)). Human fMRI shows more similar voxel time courses and higher autocorrelation in anterior than posterior hippocampus [abstract] ([Brunec et al. 2018](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.cub.2018.05.016)) and coarse-grained position encoding anteriorly [abstract] ([Evensmoen et al. 2015](https://doi.org/10.1002/hipo.22357)). A six-scale distance task shifted activity from posterior to anterior hippocampus as scale grew, but only 6 of 19 individuals showed it and a reviewer disputed the hippocampal result [full text] ([Peer et al. 2019](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6739872)). In tufted titmice, place-cell incidence rose from under 10% at the posterior pole to over 70% at the anterior pole [full text] ([Payne, Lynch & Aronov 2021](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8503942/)).

The studies disagree in three ways. Ventral precision is "comparable" to dorsal at matched population size in one mouse study and coarser in another, while rats show lower resolution. Ventral field counts are lower per environment in Maurer, equal in Keinath and spread over many arms in Royer, while Kjelstrup et al. read ventral phase precession on the 18 m track as showing one long field per cell. Whether large fields are a long-axis property or a per-cell property is also contested, because Harland et al. and Eliav et al. both find a wide range of field sizes within single dorsal CA1 cells. Harland et al. write that the dorsal-small, ventral-large concept "is challenged" by this, while Eliav et al. call the within-cell multiscale code "a fundamentally different phenomenon" from the gradient [full text] ([Harland et al. 2021](https://par.nsf.gov/servlets/purl/10294326); [Eliav et al. 2021](https://www.weizmann.ac.il/brain-sciences/labs/ulanovsky/sites/brain-sciences.labs.ulanovsky/files/2024-11/Eliav2021.pdf)). A review proposes gradients superimposed on discrete domains defined by gene expression [abstract] ([Strange et al. 2014](https://doi.org/10.1038/nrn3785)). No study gives field counts per cell in ventral hippocampus in a large environment.

**Full citations.** Kjelstrup KB, Solstad T, Brun VH, Hafting T, Leutgeb S, Witter MP, Moser EI, Moser MB (2008). Finite scale of spatial representation in the hippocampus. ## Field widths stay within about two smallest grid spacings except in large rat arenas and on the 18 m track

Dividing a place field's width by the spacing of one entorhinal grid module gives a number without units. Grid spacing changes little between environments, so the ratio moves mainly with the field. The two tables below pool every audited field width by region, long-axis level and environment class. The rat classes are real tracks up to 3 m, real arenas up to 4 m², and large real spaces, meaning tracks over 3 m or arenas over 4 m². The mouse classes are real environments up to 2 m, virtual tracks up to 3 m, and large spaces over 3 m or 1.2 m², and every mouse value in the large class comes from a virtual track. A row runs from the smallest to the largest value among its studies, and its label gives the number of studies. Columns are grid modules ordered from smallest to largest spacing. Each cell runs from the narrowest field over the widest spacing to the widest field over the narrowest spacing. The widths come from two sweeps, 82 rat rows from 30 studies and 91 mouse rows from 52 studies, and independent audits re-checked 72 of the rat rows and 80 of the mouse rows against the papers. Two later sweeps add retrosplenial and non-grid MEC rows to the mouse grid under their own headers. They hold 66 retrosplenial rows from 28 studies and 32 non-grid MEC rows from 19 studies, and audits checked every row (sweep_fields_rsc.csv, sweep_fields_mec_nongrid.csv, audit_sweep_rsc.md, audit_sweep_mec.md and page_build_rsc_mec.md). The literature notes hold the tables and checks in sweep_fields_rat.csv, sweep_fields_mouse.csv, audit_sweep_fields_rat.md and audit_sweep_fields_mouse.md, the grouping decisions in page_build_sweeps.md, and the module spacings in grid_ratio_rat_grid.md, grid_ratio_mouse_grid.md and audit_grid_ratio.md.

Against the smallest module, rat fields in tracks up to 3 m and arenas up to 4 m² span 0.33 to 1.9 grid spacings, and mouse fields in real environments up to 2 m span 0.41 to 1.4 [derived]. Mouse fields on virtual tracks are wider, 1.0 to 2.0 spacings. On virtual tracks over 3 m, CA1 and CA2/3 fields stay at 0.86 to 1.5 spacings and dentate granule cells reach 1.8 to 2.2 [derived]. Two cases go well past two spacings. In rat arenas of 4.38 and 8.75 m², fields away from the walls reach 4.5 smallest spacings [figure-read, derived] ([Tanni, de Cothi & Barry 2022](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.cub.2022.06.046)). On the 18 m track, ventral CA3 fields span 8.4 to 14 smallest spacings, but against the ventral MEC spacing measured on the same track they span 1.1 to 2.1 [derived] ([Kjelstrup et al. 2008](https://doi.org/10.1126/science.1157086); [Brun et al. 2008](https://doi.org/10.1002/hipo.20504)).

Three limits apply. Fields grow with the environment and grids barely change, so a row's ratios hold for its own environment class, and dorsal and ventral levels can be compared only within one class. Two rat studies record all three CA3 levels in one environment, Kjelstrup et al. on the 18 m track and Komorowski et al. in 37 cm boxes whose walls cap the fields. In mice only real environments up to 2 m hold all three CA1 levels, and no study records all three. A box cannot show a grid spacing larger than itself, so the largest rat spacing comes from a linear track alone, and no mouse spacing has been measured above about 76 cm. Field rules also differ within a row, from any firing above zero to the cell's mean rate plus one SD, so a row's range mixes rule and environment, and the source tables give each study's values.

Anatomy suggests which pairings matter most. The band of entorhinal cortex next to the rhinal fissure connects to dorsal hippocampus, the intermediate band to intermediate hippocampus, and the most medial band mainly to ventral hippocampus [full text] ([Canto, Wouterlood & Witter 2008](https://doi.org/10.1155/2008/381243)). In rats the four best-sampled modules lie within about 1.9 mm of the dorsal MEC border, the dorsal half of MEC [full text] ([Stensola et al. 2012](https://doi.org/10.1038/nature11649)). In mice the control grid cells of one study sat 386 ± 222 µm from that border [full text] ([Mallory et al. 2018](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41593-017-0055-3)). Equating the band next to the rhinal fissure with the dorsal MEC that holds the smallest modules takes one inference across papers [inference].

The rows follow four rules. A study gives one value per cell group, environment and statistic. Where a study reports per-field and summed widths for the same cells, the row uses the per-field value. Where it reports several measures of the same cells, such as Kjelstrup et al.'s mean largest field and autocorrelation width, Tanni et al.'s near-wall, central and long-axis widths in one arena, or Hayman et al.'s minor and major axes, the measures form that study's range. Rows leave out widths with no environment class, unverifiable values, widths the audit cleared, maximum extents, coverage upper bounds and, in mice, field rules that keep only the peak of a field. The source tables name each excluded row and its reason, and page_build_sweeps.md lists every one, including rows that carry only counts.

M1 to M5 come from Stensola et al.'s figures, whose values were read from the vector graphics; the positions come from one rat [figure-read] ([Stensola et al. 2012](https://doi.org/10.1038/nature11649)). M1 to M4 use the five data sets with four modules, where module rank is clear. First modules in other data sets go down to 32.6 cm [figure-read], and single cells to 35.2 cm [full text]. The authors report "some uncertainty about the fifth module" [full text]. The mean step between modules is 1.42 [full text], but single steps run from 1.14 to 1.78 [figure-read, derived] ([Stensola et al. 2012](https://doi.org/10.1038/nature11649)). Brun et al. recorded grid cells on the same 18 m track as Kjelstrup et al., in different rats by their identification numbers, and report each cell's smallest and median distance between neighbouring fields, averaged within three levels of the 3.5 mm MEC axis [full text; rats derived] ([Brun et al. 2008](https://doi.org/10.1002/hipo.20504)). Missed fields inflate both measures, and the authors suggest the smallest distance may be closer to the true spacing. Intermediate and ventral levels did not differ significantly, P = 0.20 for the smallest and 0.16 for the median distance. The abstract calls ~50 cm an average, but the discussion gives ~50 cm as the smallest spacing estimate near the dorsal border, read from the per-cell scatterplots, while the dorsal group mean is 91 cm [full text; link inferred]. The study's boxes only screened cells, so it reports no box spacing [full text] ([Brun et al. 2008](https://doi.org/10.1002/hipo.20504)). These track columns are 1D distances on the same apparatus as Kjelstrup's recordings, so they are filled only for the three 18 m rows. Paired by level name, CA3 width over MEC spacing on that track is 0.57 to 1.2 dorsally, 1.0 to 1.8 at the intermediate level and 1.1 to 2.1 ventrally [derived]. The pairing is an assumption: the MEC levels are 0 to 25%, 25 to 50% and 50 to 75% of the MEC axis, the CA3 track cells sit at 7 to 19%, 55 to 70% and 71 to 87% of the CA3 axis, and neither paper maps one axis onto the other [inference]. No rat study we could read gives a ventral CA1 field width. Jung et al.'s "ventral" CA1 sits at about 65% of the axis by the authors' estimate, so it appears here as intermediate [full text] ([Jung, Wiener & McNaughton 1994](https://doi.org/10.1523/JNEUROSCI.14-12-07347.1994)).

| Module | Spacing | Low end | High end | Status |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| M1 | 30 to 36 cm | 30 cm, the smallest cell at the dorsal edge of one mouse's imaged area ([Zong et al. 2022](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.cell.2022.02.017)) | 36.26 ± 9.27 cm (SD), mean of 37 control grid cells in 12 mice ([Mallory et al. 2018](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41593-017-0055-3)) | full text |
| M2, low end derived | 42 to 60 cm | 42 cm, 30 cm times 1.4 | About 60 cm, the ventral edge of the same imaged area ([Zong et al. 2022](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.cell.2022.02.017)). A control mean of 59.0 cm in a 70 cm box agrees ([Gil et al. 2018](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41593-017-0039-3)) | derived low end, full text high end |
| M3, inferred from 1D firing | 65 to 76 cm | 65 cm | 76 cm. Both are 2D spacings inferred from virtual-track firing, larger than the 75 cm box used ([Wen et al. 2024](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41586-024-08034-3)) | full text, inferred |

Mouse modules step up by 1.51 on average [full text] ([Gu et al. 2018](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.cell.2018.08.066)) and by 1.4 to 1.7 [full text] ([Wen et al. 2024](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41586-024-08034-3)), both inferred from 1D virtual-track firing. A study of dorsomedial MEC found a mean spacing of 72.5 ± 13.7 cm across 103 grid cells in 9 mice, and "no grid cells were found with grid spacings <40 cm" [full text] ([Clark & Nolan 2024](https://doi.org/10.7554/eLife.89356)). Its tetrodes started 1.2 to 1.4 mm deep and its mean pools several modules, so it is a cross-check inside M3, not a column. Grids in virtual open arenas were 1.42 times their real-arena scale [full text] ([Chen et al. 2018](https://doi.org/10.7554/eLife.34789)), so the virtual-track rows and M3 may not pair exactly with real-arena spacings. No mouse open field we found is larger than about 1 m, so ventral mouse spacing is unmeasured, and no study gives intermediate or ventral mouse CA3 field widths. Fields on virtual tracks up to 3 m run 37 to 60 cm, against 14.8 to 42.5 cm in real environments up to 2 m, and they do not widen further on longer virtual tracks. They are 45 to 46.4 cm on tracks of 4 to 41 m, and 30.9 cm on circular tracks of 3.14 to 5.03 m, where the width is the span of the strong calcium events on each lap rather than a thresholded field [derived; figure-read] ([Navratilova et al. 2026](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC13092332)). The rows leave out field rules that keep only the top of a field, which give 7 to 9 cm in mice. These are bins at least 1.5 SD above the mean ([Chockanathan & Padmanabhan 2021](https://doi.org/10.1523/ENEURO.0211-21.2021); [Schuette et al. 2022](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41598-022-13799-6)), 30 to 33% of the maximum on 4 cm bins with no smoothing ([Sheintuch et al. 2023](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.celrep.2023.112119); [Rechavi et al. 2022](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.celrep.2022.111695)) and Gaussian-mixture splits ([Tarcsay et al. 2025](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.celrep.2025.116682)) [full text]. Under the first rule, dorsal and ventral CA1 fields on a virtual track were 9.4 and 11.9 cm, not different, P = 0.84 [full text] ([Chockanathan & Padmanabhan 2021](https://doi.org/10.1523/ENEURO.0211-21.2021)).

The mouse grid ends with the retrosplenial and non-grid MEC groups. Retrosplenial fields span 0.69 to 1.6 smallest grid spacings, and 0.33 at the lowest against any module [derived]. Dysgranular RSC gives 0.69 to 1.3 smallest spacings on 1.5 m belts and a 1.57 m wheel and 1.1 to 1.3 on 3 to 5 m virtual tracks, while granular and deep-layer medians from one study give 1.3 to 1.6 [derived]. The non-grid MEC field of Mallory et al. is 1.6 to 1.9 M1 spacings across the column's range. Against the 36.26 cm spacing of the grid cells recorded in the same mice, which sets the column's high end, it is 1.55 [derived] ([Mallory et al. 2018](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41593-017-0055-3)). Border width runs perpendicular to the wall and is not a field diameter, so it has its own row, 0.40 to 0.48 spacings [derived]. That study's arena size is in its paywalled methods, so both rows lack an environment class. They stay, unlike the same study's CA1 width in the hippocampal rows, because they are the only non-grid widths and their grid spacing comes from the same mice [inference]. The rat grid has no retrosplenial row. Its only width, 36.2 cm on a plus maze of unstated size, is an equal-area diameter on narrow arms [full text, derived] ([Smith, Barredo & Mizumori 2012](https://doi.org/10.1002/hipo.20958)). The retrosplenial sweep's CA1 rows that repeat mouse-sweep rows, from Mao et al. 2017 and Lenkey et al. 2025, are left out, and its one new CA1 width joins the CA1 row for virtual tracks over 3 m. The sections on retrosplenial cortex and non-grid MEC cells further down give the context for these rows.

## Population kernels span 0.3 to 1.4 smallest grid spacings up to about 4 m and about two MEC spacings in ventral CA3

The second group of heat maps divides the population kernel by the same modules. Here the kernel width is the full width, both sides of a position, at which the correlation between population vectors falls to 0.5. Each row is one study, region, long-axis level and condition, taken from sweep_kernel.csv and its audit, audit_sweep_kernel.md, in the literature notes. The two Mao et al. 2017 rows, mouse CA1 and dysgranular retrosplenial cortex on a 1.5 m treadmill loop, carry the values of the retrosplenial audit, audit_sweep_rsc.md, and the retrosplenial row sits under its own header. Where the sweep and the audit read a figure differently, the row spans both readings. Kjelstrup et al. and Resnik et al. print their values. No study has measured a kernel and grid spacing in the same animals, so every cell pairs different animals.

In spaces up to about 4 m, rodent kernels are 17 to 54 cm wide, 0.34 to 1.4 smallest grid spacings [derived]. The widest, 54 cm, comes from a dot product without mean subtraction ([Terrazas et al. 2005](https://doi.org/10.1523/JNEUROSCI.0693-05.2005)). Without that row the range is 17 to 45 cm, 0.34 to 1.2 spacings [derived]. Mouse dorsal CA1 gives 18 to 27 cm, 0.50 to 0.90 spacings [derived]. Mouse retrosplenial cortex gives 26.4 to 26.5 cm, 0.73 to 0.88 M1 spacings, against 0.74 to 0.89 for CA1 on the same belt [derived] ([Mao et al. 2017](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5557927)). On the 18 m track the dorsal CA3 kernel, 0.81 m, is 1.7 to 2.1 smallest box spacings but 0.47 to 0.89 of the dorsal MEC spacing measured on the same track. Against the MEC spacing of the same-named level, intermediate CA3 gives 1.2 to 1.8 and ventral CA3 1.8 to 2.5 [derived] ([Kjelstrup et al. 2008](https://doi.org/10.1126/science.1157086); [Brun et al. 2008](https://doi.org/10.1002/hipo.20504)). This pairing of levels across two papers is the same inference as for the field widths.

The grids leave out four kinds of rows, and the region tables of the second section keep them all. Marmoset, bird and human kernels have no grid spacing measured in their species. Brun et al.'s grid-cell kernels come from MEC, not hippocampus; their full width at r = 0.5 is 0.16 to 0.19 of the median grid spacing on the track at all three levels [full text, derived] ([Brun et al. 2008](https://doi.org/10.1002/hipo.20504)). Sessions that change the animal or its movement are out: GluA1 knockout and Dp(16)1Yey mice, a scopolamine session and the car-driving condition of Terrazas et al. Blair et al.'s cross-half design is out because its diagonal is 0.58, so r = 0.5 sits near the peak rather than at half of it [full text, figure-read] ([Blair et al. 2023](https://doi.org/10.7554/eLife.80661)). Fischer et al.'s landmark-centred retrosplenial curves are out for a similar reason. Split halves put their peak at 0.76 to 0.82, so r = 0.5 lies at 61 to 66% of the peak, and the width changes along the track [full text, figure-read; derived] ([Fischer et al. 2020](https://doi.org/10.7554/eLife.51458)). Mao et al.'s layer subsets and colour-read Thy1 matrices are in the retrosplenial section, and two rat retrosplenial curves have no width. Each curve enters once. Mao et al.'s two curves appear in both sweep_kernel.csv and sweep_kernel_rsc.csv, and Brun et al.'s three in both sweep_kernel.csv and sweep_kernel_mec.csv.

## Fields per cell per grid period are 0.025 to 0.036 on long tracks, counting every cell

Each cell has its own field rate. Given that rate, the number of fields a cell has in a space is Poisson, and across cells the rates follow a gamma distribution with mean μ and shape k. Rich et al. fitted this model on the 48 m track, Tanni et al. found it better than equal rates across four arenas, and Lee et al.'s preprint reports the same on a 41 m virtual path [full text; preprint]. μ needs a length unit. The table uses the smallest grid module, rat M1 at 39 to 49 cm and mouse M1 at 30 to 36 cm, and carries that range. In one dimension μ is fields per grid period, the rate per metre times the spacing λ. In two dimensions it is fields per λ², the rate per square metre times λ². k has no unit, and a small k means a few cells hold most of the fields. Counts in small spaces overstate μ, because a cell enters the count only if it has a field there, so where a study gives two or more sizes the table estimates μ from the slope of count against size. Five datasets count cells without fields: Rich et al. found silent cells in sleep sessions before and after the track, Lee et al. counted the 27.7% of imaged cells with no field, Grieves et al. the 87 of 628 arena cells with no field, Zhao et al. all putative pyramidal cells, and Jung et al. all complex-spike cells. Among the retrosplenial rows, Navratilova et al. give fields per metre over all imaged cells, and the place-cell shares of Mao et al. and Esteves et al. allow the same conversion. Every other row counts only cells with a field or does not say. The arithmetic for every derived number is in page_build_sweeps.md and, for the retrosplenial and non-grid MEC rows at the end of the table, page_build_rsc_mec.md.
