The two long-track datasets that count every cell agree across species. Rich et al.'s 48 m track gives 0.029 to 0.036 fields per rat grid period and Lee et al.'s 41 m virtual path 0.025 to 0.030 per mouse grid period [derived]. In two dimensions the slope across Tanni et al.'s arenas gives 0.020 to 0.031 fields per λ² for place cells [derived]. Kjelstrup et al.'s place cells on the 18 m track give 0.035 to 0.050 per period, from counts the authors call overestimates [derived]. Every estimate from a single space of up to 4.5 m that counts only active cells or cells with a field is higher, from 0.076 per λ² in a 2 m cylinder to 1.7 in a 41 cm square, and slopes between two small enclosures stay high too, 0.18 to 0.51 per λ² [derived]. Chen et al.'s place cells in 8.9 m mazes, with an absolute 0.2 Hz threshold, give 0.23 to 0.27 per period [derived]. The spread across cells also differs. k is 0.57 for Rich et al.'s rats and 0.76 to 0.89 for Lee et al.'s mice [derived], so a few cells hold most fields. Chen et al.'s counts give k of 6 to 8 [derived], but they cover only cells with fields. Leaving out cells without fields raises a moment estimate of k; on Lee et al.'s histogram it doubles, from 0.89 to 1.86 [derived]. Grieves et al.'s single-arena counts are underdispersed, with variance 0.48 times the mean, which no gamma-Poisson model can produce, so that row gives a rate but no k.

Retrosplenial counts over all imaged cells sit close to the long-track hippocampal values. Navratilova et al.'s 3 to 5 m virtual tracks give 0.028 to 0.033 fields per mouse grid period in the familiar room and 0.018 to 0.021 in the novel one, against 0.044 to 0.053 and 0.024 to 0.029 for CA1 in the same study [derived]. Mao et al.'s 1.5 m belt gives 0.041 to 0.049 [derived]. Esteves et al.'s Thy1 mice give 0.10 to 0.13, from a share of 41 to 46% under liberal criteria [figure-read, derived]. Counting only place cells raises the three studies to 0.072 to 0.37 [derived]. Non-grid MEC cells in boxes of about 1 m² have 0.17 to 0.26 fields per λ² and border cells 0.14 to 0.21, within the range of single-space hippocampal counts over cells with a field [derived]. The lower MEC rates per m² assume 1 m² boxes. Weighting in the 47 sessions in smaller boxes lowers the mean area to 0.95 m² and gives the higher rates.

## CA3 fields are smaller than CA1 in rats and bats but not in mice, and CA2 fields are larger

Within rat dorsal hippocampus, same-animal comparisons give a consistent ordering. CA3 fields are more compact than CA1 fields and carry more information per spike, 1.77 against 1.31 bits/spike in the open field and 1.57 against 0.83 on the track, and fewer CA3 cells have fields on a track, 16.8% against 48.0% with a coherence criterion [full text] ([Mizuseki et al. 2012](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3718552/)). Along the CA3 transverse axis, proximal CA3 near the dentate gyrus has smaller, sharper fields than distal CA3 and CA2 [abstract] ([Lu et al. 2015](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.neuron.2015.07.007)). In 80 × 80 cm and 50 cm radius enclosures, **CA2 fields were 24.5% larger than CA1 fields**, z = -2.54, P = 0.034, CA2 cells had more fields than CA1 and CA3 cells, and CA2 carried less spatial information, P < 0.001. Active fractions per session were 59.2% in CA1, 68.2% in CA2 and 48.1% in CA3 [full text] ([Mankin et al. 2015](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4392894/)). Bats show the CA3-CA1 contrast at a much larger scale, with CA3 maps sparser, higher in spatial information and lower in coverage than CA1 maps in 130 to 200 m tunnels, p = 10⁻⁷² for information [full text] ([Maimon et al. 2026](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC13421320)). Mice do not show the contrast in field size. In the same mice, CA3 fields covered 22.9% of a 50 cm box and 27.4% of a 100 cm track, against 20.8% and 24.8% for CA1 [full text] ([Hussaini et al. 2011](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.neuron.2011.09.007)). On a familiar 3 m virtual track, CA3a fields were wider than CA1 fields, medians about 44 against 38 cm, P = 7.1 × 10⁻⁴ [figure-read; P value full text] ([Dong et al. 2021](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41467-021-23260-3)). On a 4 m virtual track, CA2/3 and CA1 widths did not differ, medians about 40 and 45 cm [figure-read] ([Hainmueller & Bartos 2018](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41586-018-0191-2)).

The dentate gyrus contains two cell types with opposite statistics. In rats recorded in four rooms with platforms about 107 to 117 cm across, granule-cell-layer cells had **1.04 fields per active cell** and were active in 9% of environments on average, while hilar putative mossy cells had 2.26 fields and were active in 88%, and CA3 cells had 1.19 fields and were active in 29%. All 8 juxtacellularly identified granule cells had a single field [full text] ([GoodSmith et al. 2017](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5300955/)). In 16 mice with 586 putative granule cells and 500 putative mossy cells, only 6.6% of recorded granule cells had two or more fields, against 57.8% of mossy cells and 36.1% of CA3 cells, and granule cells had the lowest spatial information [full text] ([Senzai & Buzsáki 2017](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5293146/)). Earlier work had separated single-field cells, proposed as mature granule cells, from multi-field cells below the granule layer [abstract] ([Neunuebel & Knierim 2012](https://doi.org/10.1523/JNEUROSCI.6038-11.2012)). The 5.35 fields per DG cell in Park et al.'s large box probably include mossy cells, though Park et al. did not separate the types [inference] ([Park, Dvorak & Fenton 2011](https://journals.plos.org/plosone/article?id=10.1371/journal.pone.0022349)).

CA1 itself varies along two axes. Along the proximodistal axis in a 200 cm cylinder, distal cells had more fields on the first trial, 2.13 against 1.57 proximally, and fewer single-field cells, 33.8% against 57.0%, without larger fields, P = 0.19, while spatial information fell distally [full text] ([Henriksen et al. 2010](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3093538/)). Deep-layer CA1 cells were "more likely to have place fields" and fired at higher rates [abstract] ([Mizuseki et al. 2011](https://doi.org/10.1038/nn.2894)). Superficial maps were more stable while deep maps stabilized during goal learning [abstract] ([Danielson et al. 2016](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.neuron.2016.06.020)), and superficial cells were more active in cue-poor environments while deep cells were more active in cue-rich ones [abstract] ([Sharif et al. 2021](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.neuron.2020.10.034)). These abstracts do not report field size by sublayer.

Structures outside the hippocampus proper carry weaker or differently formatted spatial codes. In rat caudodorsal lateral septum on a T maze, 26.5% of cells were place cells against 63.0% in simultaneously recorded CA1, with field length **28.8 ± 16.6 cm against 29.1 ± 14.7 cm**, about 1.7 and 1.5 fields per place cell, and 0.73 against 1.54 bits/spike [full text, field counts derived] ([Wirtshafter & Wilson 2020](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7274787/)). A larger silicon-probe study found place fields in 5.3% of 4,393 lateral septum rate maps against 31.7% of 5,735 hippocampal maps. Lateral septal neurons carried position mainly in theta phase, which decoded position better than rate in 786 of 840 neurons [full text] ([Tingley & Buzsáki 2018](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6605060/)). About 95% of medial entorhinal cells passed spatial cutoffs, and of the spatial cells 17.7% were grid cells, 9.3% border cells and 67.5% non-grid spatial cells, whose field sizes were not reported [full text] ([Diehl et al. 2017](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5444540/)). One mouse study does report them [full text] ([Mallory et al. 2018](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41593-017-0055-3)). Lateral entorhinal neurons showed weak spatial specificity [abstract] ([Hargreaves et al. 2005](https://doi.org/10.1126/science.1110449)). In mouse retrosplenial cortex on a 150 cm treadmill, 297 of 2,256 AAV-labelled neurons, about 13%, were place cells. Their fields were 39.6 ± 1.2 cm wide (mean ± s.e.m., edge at 20% of peak above baseline), against 37.0 ± 0.8 cm in CA1 imaged the same way [full text] ([Mao et al. 2017](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5557927)). Rostral thalamic nuclei including reuniens contain place-like, head-direction and border-like units [abstract] ([Jankowski et al. 2015](https://doi.org/10.3389/fnbeh.2015.00256)). Medial prefrontal studies disagree, with a "substantial proportion" of prelimbic and infralimbic cells having goal-biased fields in one lab [abstract] ([Hok et al. 2005](https://doi.org/10.1073/pnas.0407332102)) and "very few" place-specific deep-layer cells in another [abstract] ([Jung et al. 1998](https://doi.org/10.1093/cercor/8.5.437)). No source gives field sizes in metres for nucleus reuniens or prefrontal cortex. Mouse retrosplenial place fields on 1.5 to 5 m tracks are 25 to 50 cm wide and close to CA1 fields measured the same way: 37.31 cm in both regions on a 1.57 m wheel ([Lenkey et al. 2025](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41467-025-60679-4)), and about 40 against 31 cm on 3 to 5 m virtual tracks, a gap that disappears when the mouse line is matched ([Navratilova et al. 2026](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC13092332)) [full text, figure-read]. The sections on retrosplenial cortex and on non-grid MEC cells, after the subiculum section, give these regions in full.

## Bats, primates and birds share place coding at very different rates

Bats give the clearest rodent-like code outside rodents, and the highest place-cell rates. In the 200 m tunnel, 83.4% of dorsal CA1 putative pyramidal cells were place cells [full text] ([Eliav et al. 2021](https://www.weizmann.ac.il/brain-sciences/labs/ulanovsky/sites/brain-sciences.labs.ulanovsky/files/2024-11/Eliav2021.pdf)), and pooled over 130 to 200 m tunnels, 76.3% of CA1 and 58.6% of CA3 pyramidal cells were [full text] ([Maimon et al. 2026](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC13421320)). In three-dimensional flight, over 90% of neurons encoded all three axes with similar resolution, so fields were close to isotropic [abstract] ([Yartsev & Ulanovsky 2013](https://doi.org/10.1126/science.1235338)). In crawling bats, 47 of 64 non-diffuse cells, 73%, were place cells [unverified, snippet] ([Ulanovsky & Moss 2007](https://www.weizmann.ac.il/brain-sciences/labs/ulanovsky/sites/neurobiology.labs.ulanovsky/files/uploads/ulanovsky_moss_natneurosci2007.pdf)). In a flight room, 27% of 304 CA1 neurons were spatially selective for current position but 46% were selective at some time lag, and for 42% of those the current-position information was negligible, so analyses of current position miss nonlocal coding [full text] ([Dotson & Yartsev 2021](https://doi.org/10.1126/science.abg1278)).

Primates show fewer place cells and stronger coding of view or facing direction. In 2 freely moving marmosets, 289 of 2,054 single and multi-units, **14.1%**, were place cells, with about 1.22 directional fields per place cell and 1.60 ± 1.61 bits/spike. Theta came in bouts of about 0.4 s and was not consistently coupled to place-cell firing [full text, fields per cell derived] ([Courellis et al. 2019](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6922474/)). In 3 rhesus macaques foraging in a 3.30 m diameter arena, 26% of hippocampal units passed a classical place test at 1 bit/spike, but only 7% remained place-tuned once facing location, head direction and other variables were fit. Facing location tuned 24% of cells and spatial view 11%, and grid tuning was about 1 to 2% [full text] ([Mao et al. 2021](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8571030/)). In a virtual X-maze, macaque hippocampal position codes did not generalize across two tasks in the same environment [abstract] ([Gulli et al. 2020](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41593-019-0548-3)). Earlier macaque work emphasized spatial view cells, whose responses depend on where the monkey looks [abstract, review] ([Rolls & Xiang 2006](https://doi.org/10.1515/REVNEURO.2006.17.1-2.175)).

Human single units come only from epilepsy patients navigating virtual environments, so no human field has a physical size. Location-specific cells were found mainly in hippocampus among 317 neurons in a virtual town [abstract] ([Ekstrom et al. 2003](https://doi.org/10.1038/nature01964)). Secondary sources report 24% of hippocampal neurons as pure place cells with about 1.7 fields each, which could not be checked [secondary, unverified]. In another virtual town, 95 of 371 units, 25.6%, were place-responsive [full text, annotated copy] ([Miller et al. 2013](https://www.scienceintheclassroom.org/research-papers/original-gps-how-we-remember-what-happened-where)). Entorhinal "path cells" coded direction of travel around a virtual square road [abstract] ([Jacobs et al. 2010](https://doi.org/10.1073/pnas.0911213107)).

Birds have mammal-like place cells whose prevalence tracks ecology. In tufted titmice, a food-caching species, 321 of 538 excitatory cells, **60%**, were place cells, against 48 of 179, 27%, in non-caching zebra finches [full text] ([Payne, Lynch & Aronov 2021](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8503942/)). In flying barn owls in a 4 × 2.2 × 2.4 m room, 27% of hippocampal units and 48% of hyperpallium apicale units were place cells, with field size not differing across regions [full text] ([Agarwal et al. 2023](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC9945993/)). A chickadee study reportedly found 56% place cells among 4,366 units [unverified] ([Chettih et al. 2024](https://www.cell.com/cell/fulltext/S0092-8674(24)00235-6)). Field sizes in metres were not available for marmosets on the L-track, for macaques, for birds or for 3D bat recordings. Cross-species comparisons of place-cell rates are weak because bat and bird papers report fractions of cells that fired, primate studies often do not control for view, and humans navigate on screens [inference].

## The subiculum spreads firing over half the space yet decodes as well as CA1

The subiculum is the main output of the hippocampus and the clearest real-data case of broad single-unit spatial tuning in rodents. Its cells fire at several times the CA1 rate, carry far less information per spike, spread their firing over roughly twice the area, and still support population decoding at least as good as CA1.

### Subicular cells fire faster and more broadly, with less information per spike

The early descriptions are qualitative. Barnes et al. found that neither entorhinal cortex nor subiculum "exhibited the highly localized patterns of spatial firing observed in the CA fields", that rates there were "substantially higher", and that some subicular cells showed "spatially consistent, but dispersed, firing … suggestive of the convergence of a number of CA1 place cells" [abstract] ([Barnes et al. 1990](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/2392566/)). Sharp and Green reported that "most subicular cells showed a robust locational signal" but "tended to fire throughout much of the environment", with "graded, location-related rate modulation" rather than compact fields. They recorded 99 dorsal subicular cells in 21 rats chasing pellets in a 76 cm cylinder and kept every cell they could isolate. On average a cell fired at least one spike in 84% of the visited 2.9 cm pixels, a figure that includes 10 putative interneurons firing in 99%, and bursting and non-bursting cells did so in 70% and 79%. Spatial coherence was significant for 83 of the 99 cells. Its mean was 0.48, against 0.80 for hippocampal place cells recorded in the same apparatus in an earlier study that kept only cells with visible fields, and 11% of subicular cells reached 0.80. Head direction was significant for 60 of 98 cells but explained 1% of rate variance on average and 13% at most. The authors recorded no hippocampal cells, did not count fields and did not compute spatial information [full text] ([Sharp & Green 1994](https://doi.org/10.1523/JNEUROSCI.14-04-02339.1994)). Later studies that recorded subiculum and CA1 in the same animals put numbers on this.

| Study | Animals, method | Environment | Mean rate, SUB vs CA1 | Spatial information, SUB vs CA1 | Spread of firing, SUB vs CA1 | Fields |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Kim, Ganguli & Frank 2012 | 5 rats, tetrodes, 91 SUB units | Two 350 cm U-tracks | Medians 2.5 proximal, 6.3 middle, 10.3 distal vs 1.4 Hz | 0.057 vs 0.021 bits per 20 ms bin | Spatial activity fraction 0.31, 0.54, 0.69 vs 0.26 | "a majority … have multiple, irregularly spaced" fields |
| Ledergerber et al. 2021 | 8 rats, tetrodes, 421 SUB and 325 CA1 cells | 150 × 150 cm box | 4.44 vs 1.7 Hz | 0.11 vs 0.84 bits/spike; 0.42 vs 0.47 bits/s | 95% of spikes in 56.3% vs 25.7% of bins | Not reported |
| Kitanishi, Umaba & Mizuseki 2021 | 11 rats, 256-channel probes, 319 SUB and 315 CA1 cells | 234 cm track, 118 cm open field, T-maze, zigzag | 9.8 vs 3.7 Hz on track | 0.34 vs 0.94 bits/spike; 1.39 vs 1.43 bits/s | Not reported | Place-cell share did not differ, χ² = 0.02 |
| Lee, Lee & Lee 2018 | 7 rats, tetrodes, 242 SUB and 129 CA1 units | T-maze, 73 cm stem, 38 cm arms | Higher in SUB, Z = 6.83 | 0.25 vs 1.12 bits/spike | Skaggs sparsity 0.78 vs 0.43 | CA1 over 80% single-field; SUB about half multi-field; SUB single fields larger, Z = 8.09 |
| Simonnet & Brecht 2019 | 40 rats, juxtacellular, 84 cells | 70 × 70 cm arena | 13.6 and 20.1 Hz for two burst classes | Medians 0.19 and 0.06 bits/spike, all cells | Not reported | 63% spatially modulated |
| Abad-Perez et al. 2025 | 11 mice, tetrodes and 64-channel probe | 50 to 80 cm arenas | 1.8 vs 1.2 Hz | 0.32 vs 0.43 bits/spike | Field 30.0% vs 29.0% of arena, p = 0.528 | Mostly proximal SUB sampled |

All rows are [full text] ([Kim, Ganguli & Frank 2012](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3458125); [Ledergerber et al. 2021](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8170370); [Kitanishi, Umaba & Mizuseki 2021](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7946376); [Lee, Lee & Lee 2018](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6596140); [Simonnet & Brecht 2019](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6510334); [Abad-Perez et al. 2025](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12685833)). Kim et al. say their spatial activity fraction "can be interpreted as the proportion of the environment over which a neuron fires". On the 350 cm track that is about 242 cm for distal subiculum against 91 cm for CA1 [derived]. Ledergerber's 56% of a 150 cm box is an area equal to a square about 113 cm on a side, against 76 cm for CA1 [derived]. These measures differ from each other and from a thresholded field, so they should not be ranked as one quantity. Taken together, subicular information per spike is **3 to 8 times lower** than in CA1 while information per second is about equal, so subicular cells spend more spikes for the same information. The broad-tuning picture holds most strongly in rats and in middle and distal subiculum. A mouse study sampling mostly proximal subiculum found no difference in field size [full text] ([Abad-Perez et al. 2025](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12685833)), and 68.5% of mouse subicular neurons were place cells in arenas of 30 to 35 cm [full text] ([Sun et al. 2024](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10972755)). No study reports subicular field size in centimetres with a stated threshold alongside CA1, and none gives a distribution of fields per cell.

### Distal subiculum is broadest and proximal subiculum is close to CA1

Kim et al. found a strong proximodistal gradient, with spatial activity fraction rising from 0.31 to 0.69 and rate from 2.5 to 10.3 Hz, p < 10⁻⁴, and describe proximal cells as "more similar to canonical, sparsely firing hippocampal place cells" [full text] ([Kim, Ganguli & Frank 2012](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3458125)). Sharp and Green had seen only "slightly higher" rates, spatial signalling and field size distally. Distal against proximal cells, 52 against 34, averaged 11.2 against 6.3 Hz, spatial coherence 0.59 against 0.38, and firing in 88% against 78% of visited pixels. With cells as samples, the coherence, field-size and in-field rate differences were significant, at p < 0.001, < 0.01 and < 0.05, and the overall rate difference was not. With the means of 16 distal and 9 proximal electrode tracks as samples, none was significant [full text] ([Sharp & Green 1994](https://doi.org/10.1523/JNEUROSCI.14-04-02339.1994)). Lee et al. found the opposite on one property, with multi-field units more common proximally, p = 0.033 [full text] ([Lee, Lee & Lee 2018](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6596140)). The two need not conflict, because a cell can fire at graded rates almost everywhere without forming discrete fields [inference]. Vector trace cells were 36% of cue-responsive cells distally against 5% proximally [full text] ([Poulter et al. 2021](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7116739)). Gene expression splits the subiculum into proximal and distal classes with different projection targets, and distal subiculum was needed for encoding in a spatial working memory task [secondary] ([Cembrowski et al. 2018, news summary](https://www.sciencedaily.com/releases/2018/04/180419131055.htm)). Burst firing does not predict broader tuning. Sparsely bursting cells were more often spatial than dominantly bursting cells, 67% against 33%, p = 0.022, and all 17 boundary cells were sparsely bursting [full text] ([Simonnet & Brecht 2019](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6510334)). In a 76 cm cylinder, bursting and non-bursting cells had the same rate, about 4 Hz, and similar coherence, 0.44 and 0.46, but non-bursting cells fired over more of the floor, 79% against 70% of pixels, a significant difference [full text] ([Sharp & Green 1994](https://doi.org/10.1523/JNEUROSCI.14-04-02339.1994)). Ventral subicular fields were reported larger than CA1 fields in one study known only from a catalogue record [unverified].

### Boundary, trace, corner and axis cells shape subicular firing

Boundary vector cells are the best-documented subicular type. In 6 rats foraging in a 62 × 62 cm box with 50 cm walls, Lever et al. found 36 such cells and put an upper bound of 24% on their share, from 45 boundary-cell recordings among 186 recorded firing fields. The 45 include 9 cells recorded again on later days, so the ratio counts recordings, not distinct cells. A second field appeared along an inserted barrier in all 3 cells tested, and 3 cells kept their firing in complete darkness. Changing the square for a 79 cm walled circle did not disrupt boundary firing, and 14 of 17 cells were "primarily insensitive" even to a 90 cm raised platform with no walls. Firing carried 0.25 bits/spike about location against 0.05 about head direction, and directional selectivity was 1.56 against 2.87 for CA1 place cells analysed the same way in an earlier study. Mean rate was 5.0 Hz, and the peak rates printed on the 36 maps run from 1.6 to 28.6 Hz, median about 11 Hz. The authors say short-range cells appear to outnumber long-range ones and that cells they grouped as far-tuned had broader fields, but they give no counts and did not measure field area [full text; peak rates figure-read] ([Lever et al. 2009](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2736390)). In a 150 cm walled circle, peaks sat 70.74 ± 1.26 cm from the centre, about 4.3 cm from the wall, and 42 of 57 cells with wall-adjacent fields gained a second field along an inserted barrier [full text, distance derived] ([Stewart et al. 2014](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3866438)). A developmental study classified 187 of 517 adult subicular neurons, 36%, as boundary vector cells, with a mean preferred distance of 7.5 ± 0.6 cm and preferred directions within ±12° of wall orientations in square boxes [full text] ([Muessig et al. 2024](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10834499)). Mouse imaging with a border-score criterion found 10.7% in a square and 7.2% in a rectangle [full text] ([Sun et al. 2024](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10972755)), so prevalence ranges from about 10% to 36% depending on species and criterion. "Boundary-off" cells fire everywhere except where a matching boundary cell fires, but only a few have been recorded [full text] ([Stewart et al. 2014](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3866438)). Vector trace cells, 73 of 253 cue-responsive cells or 29%, kept a field at a vector offset after a cue was removed, for 0.4 to 2.5 h, and were tuned 18.8 cm from the cue against 12.4 cm for other cue cells [full text] ([Poulter et al. 2021](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7116739)). Concave corner cells were 7.0 to 7.2% of mouse subicular neurons in triangles and squares and about 0% in circles, against 0.6% of CA1 neurons in a square [full text] ([Sun et al. 2024](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10972755)). Axis-tuned cells fire for travel in either direction along one axis of a multi-path track, so one cell fires on separate segments that share that axis. In 3 rats on a 160 × 125 cm track with 8 cm paths, 47 of 542 subicular neurons, 8.7%, met the criteria. Excluding one outlier, their firing at the two tuning peaks averaged 4.65 ± 2.23 times their firing at the minima. No neuron met the criteria in a 60 cm wall-less arena above the track. When the track was rotated 90°, all 26 tested axis cells kept their tuning fixed to the room, with tuning curves correlating at 0.76 in room coordinates against -0.41 in track coordinates. The paper's 73.9 ± 12.5% is not track coverage. It is a selection score averaged over the 47 cells. A cell's score is the share of well-sampled 2 cm track positions where, while the rat faced a preferred direction, it fired at least half its mean rate for that direction, and a cell needed over 50% to qualify. In the arena, about 29% of subicular cells had place-like maps and about 21% boundary-like maps by correlation with idealized templates at r > 0.4, and 7 of the 47 axis cells were boundary-like there [full text; percentage derived; supplementary figures not read] ([Olson, Tongprasearth & Nitz 2017](https://doi.org/10.1038/nn.4464)). No dedicated report of subicular object-vector cells was found, and no boundary, trace or corner cell paper reports field area.

### Subicular populations generalize across rooms, decode well and have a similarity kernel about as wide as CA1's

Subicular maps change less between environments than CA1 maps, but they do change. Sharp reported that subicular cells "showed very similar firing patterns in the two chambers", a cylinder and a square, "regardless of whether they were visually similar or different", while hippocampal cells remapped [abstract] ([Sharp 1997](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/9095343/)). In the earlier study, Sharp and Green moved rats directly from a 76 cm cylinder into a 39 × 55.5 cm rectangle while recording 8 cells. Each cell fired over much of both boxes, and 5 of the 8 appeared by eye to repeat their cylinder pattern in the same room orientation, with no quantitative test. The authors note that the hippocampal comparison studies used a home-cage interval rather than direct transfer [full text] ([Sharp & Green 1994](https://doi.org/10.1523/JNEUROSCI.14-04-02339.1994)). Subicular patterns in a large square were "expanded versions" of those in a smaller square inside it, but the expansion did not cross barriers [abstract] ([Sharp 1999](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/10495074/)), and Sharp described a "rigid framework of interrelated firing fields" fitted to each enclosure [abstract] ([Sharp 2006](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/16859764/)). On two identical-looking tracks, all 39 testable subicular cells had higher same-track than cross-track similarity, so the maps did differ. Yet mean rates across tracks correlated at **r = 0.87 in subiculum against 0.19 in CA1**, and only 4 of 59 subicular cells, 7%, were active on just one track against 7 of 15 CA1 cells, 47% [full text] ([Kim, Ganguli & Frank 2012](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3458125)). Lever et al. concluded that boundary vector cells "remap less than PCs in response to the same environmental differences". CA1 maps from 5 rats correlated at 0.803 between two trials in the square, 0.053 between the square and a walled circle, and -0.049 between the square and a wall-less platform, values the paper labels an unpublished observation. The paper gives no correlation for the boundary cells, and the change to the wall-less platform "elicited remapping in many of the non-BVC subicular cells" recorded alongside them, with data not shown [full text] ([Lever et al. 2009](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2736390)). Subicular firing was "more schematic" in a visual-scene task, with higher within-epoch and lower between-epoch population correlations, and it did not change under scene blurring while CA1 rate remapping did [full text] ([Lee, Lee & Lee 2018](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6596140)). Tuning on the left and right arms of a T-maze correlated at 0.41 in subiculum against 0.26 in CA1, P = 0.0016 [full text] ([Nakai, Kitanishi & Mizuseki 2024](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10830115)). In bats switching between vision and echolocation, subicular neurons turned on or off while CA1 neurons shifted their fields [abstract] ([Geva-Sagiv et al. 2016](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/27239936/)).

Within one environment, the subicular population code changes slightly faster with distance than the CA1 code, but only over short distances. The authors write that the mean PV autocorrelation peak was narrower in subiculum, "a faster change of the population code between nearby spatial locations" [full text]. Read from the supplement, the subicular curve falls to 0.5 at 18.6 to 19.2 cm against 22.2 to 22.5 cm in CA1, about 14% narrower, and at the smallest distance bin, 2.5 cm, it is 0.90 against 0.95. Further out the order reverses, and the subicular curve crosses zero at about 60 cm against 54 cm for CA1 [figure-read, Fig. S7H]. The open-field curves come from 5 of the 8 rats, and the one statistic in the legend, an F-test from an autoregressive model of each bin's rate, does not compare widths [full text] ([Ledergerber et al. 2021](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8170370)). Decoding is at least as good. With a 400 ms window and 5 cm bins, subiculum and CA1 did not differ at 5, 20 or 50 neurons, p = 0.66, 0.61 and 0.32, but subiculum was better at **100 neurons, p = 0.005, and 150 neurons, p < 0.001** [full text] ([Ledergerber et al. 2021](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8170370)). On a linear track, errors were comparable at all population sizes, P > 0.2, thinning subicular spikes to CA1 rates made subicular decoding worse, and subicular error grew less under added noise [full text] ([Kitanishi, Umaba & Mizuseki 2021](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7946376)). With 10 units, position R² was 0.25 in subiculum against 0.13 in CA1, P = 0.010 [full text] ([Nakai, Kitanishi & Mizuseki 2024](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10830115)). Population information grew by 0.064 bits per added distal subicular neuron against 0.036 for CA1 [full text] ([Kim, Ganguli & Frank 2012](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3458125)). Neighbouring subicular cells have unrelated maps. Pairs recorded on the same wire correlated at 0.06 on average for 4 simultaneous pairs and 0.0003 for 37 pairs from different sessions, while one cell's maps from repeated sessions correlated at 0.49, n = 7 [full text] ([Sharp & Green 1994](https://doi.org/10.1523/JNEUROSCI.14-04-02339.1994)). Subicular population activity also has higher intrinsic dimension, a correlation dimension of **5.24 against 3.07 in the T-maze and 5.84 against 3.61 in the open field**, and its manifold structure was more similar across tasks than CA1's, P = 0.0057 [full text] ([Nakai, Kitanishi & Mizuseki 2024](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10830115)). A conference poster reports that dimensionality rises with remapping less in subiculum than in CA1 and CA3 [unverified, conference abstract] ([FENS 2024 poster](https://world-wide.org/eposter/fens-24/dimensionality-neural-sub-populations-t851pjkv)).

### No subicular recording exists in a large space

The largest environment with subicular recordings is a 3.30 m macaque arena. There, 5% of 110 subicular-complex units passed a classical place test, 12% were spatially tuned in a model-based analysis, 74% were head-direction tuned, and subiculum had "the least sparsity" of the three regions sampled. The sample mixed prosubiculum, subiculum and presubiculum [full text] ([Mao et al. 2021](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8571030/)). The largest rodent environments are a 350 cm U-track [full text] ([Kim, Ganguli & Frank 2012](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3458125)), a 234 cm track [full text] ([Kitanishi, Umaba & Mizuseki 2021](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7946376)), a 150 × 190 cm box [full text] ([Poulter et al. 2021](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7116739)) and a 150 cm circle [full text] ([Stewart et al. 2014](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3866438)). In human intracranial recordings, theta during encoding was stronger for targets near boundaries, an effect specific to the subiculum, F(2,34) = 4.71, p = 0.016 [full text] ([Lee et al. 2018](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5884460)). The 56 to 69% coverage in 1.5 to 3.5 m spaces therefore cannot be extended to tens of metres, and whether subicular firing grows, fragments or stays fixed in a large space is untested.

## Retrosplenial fields and kernels match CA1 in head-fixed mice, and one rat study finds fields 1.7 times wider

An audited sweep of retrosplenial cortex (RSC) found 66 field rows from 28 studies and 11 kernel rows from 4 studies, in rats, mice and macaques (sweep_rsc.md and audit_sweep_rsc.md in the literature notes). Four studies measured RSC place fields alongside CA1 fields with one rule. Three imaged head-fixed mice with two-photon microscopy and found RSC fields about as wide as CA1 fields. The fourth recorded freely moving rats and found RSC fields larger. Field rules, mouse lines and tracks differ between studies, so the ratios below come from within each study.

### Paired studies find mouse fields close to CA1 width and rat fields wider

In mouse RSC on a 150 cm treadmill belt with tactile cues, 297 of 2,256 AAV-labelled neurons, 13%, were place cells. Their fields were 39.6 ± 1.2 cm wide (mean ± s.e.m., edge at 20% of peak above baseline), against 37.0 ± 0.8 cm for CA1 imaged the same way, P = 0.57, a ratio of 1.07. The medians were 34.5 and 31.5 cm, a ratio of 1.10 [full text; medians figure-read; ratios derived] ([Mao et al. 2017](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5557927)). In 3 of the same 4 mice, the median was 42.0 cm in superficial dysgranular RSC, 48.0 cm in the deep layers and 49.5 cm in superficial granular RSC, where only 30 place cells entered [full text, figure-read]. A later study from the same group applied the same rule to Thy1 mice and found about 25.0 cm in sham controls and 27.9 cm after bilateral hippocampal lesions, P = 0.28. The lesions cut the place-cell fraction from 0.50 to 0.29 [full text, figure-read] ([Mao et al. 2018](https://doi.org/10.1073/pnas.1803224115)).

On a 1.57 m wheel in darkness with tactile cues, Thy1 mice gave median widths of 37.31 cm in RSC, interquartile range 27.49 to 49.09 cm, and 37.31 cm in CA1, 25.53 to 49.09 cm. Field edges sat at 50% of the range, and cells with more than one peak were excluded [full text] ([Lenkey et al. 2025](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41467-025-60679-4)). The equal medians fall on the 1.96 cm bin grid, and the 50% edge is higher than Mao et al.'s 20% edge, so widths from the two studies do not compare directly [inference].

On virtual circular tracks of 3.14, 3.77 and 5.03 m, Navratilova et al. imaged 11,485 RSC cells in 18 sessions from 16 mice, 13 CaMK2a-tTA and 3 Thy1, and CA1 in 6 Thy1 mice. Their field size is the distance between the first and last of the stronger half of calcium events on each lap, averaged over laps, not a thresholded width. Read from Fig. 5E, RSC fields were about 40.2 cm in the familiar room and 38.1 cm in a novel one, against 30.9 and 32.1 cm in CA1, ratios of 1.30 and 1.19 [full text, figure-read; ratios derived]. The paper contradicts itself on whether the gap is real. The text says "RSC place fields were 19 % bigger than CA1 place fields", and Table 1 rejects equality, F = 6.2, p = 0.022, but the Fig. 5E legend says "There was no significant difference between RSC and CA1 place field sizes" [full text]. The mouse line may explain the gap. In the supplement, Thy1 mice had smaller RSC fields than CaMK2a-tTA mice, "29 cm vs 38 cm, T-test: p=0.038", and "There was no difference between Thy1 RSC mice and Thy1 CA1 mice in these measures", though only 3 Thy1 mice gave RSC data [full text] ([Navratilova et al. 2026](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC13092332)). The line matters across studies too. Mao et al.'s rule gave about 25 cm in Thy1 mice and 39.6 cm in AAV mice [derived, across two studies].

Freely moving rats give a different answer. On a plus maze, stereotrodes recorded granular RSC and CA1 in the same rats and sessions, and one rule defined fields in both regions. A field needed at least 4 adjacent 2.8 cm pixels but less than half the maze, an in-field rate at least twice the out-of-field rate, and firing on more than half the passes. RSC fields averaged 1,031.44 cm² against 368.16 cm² in CA1, 2.80 times the area. As equal-area diameters that is 36.2 against 21.7 cm, a ratio of 1.67 [full text; diameters derived] ([Smith, Barredo & Mizumori 2012](https://doi.org/10.1002/hipo.20958)). The authors write that RSC fields "often encompassed an entire arm of the maze" [full text]. The paper gives no maze size, so the fields have no share of the environment, and on narrow arms an equal-area diameter understates the length along an arm [inference]. In 1 m boxes, the median RSC sparsity index, here the share of the box over which a cell fires, was about 0.96, so most RSC cells fired over nearly the whole box [full text, figure-read] ([Subramanian, Miller & Smith 2024](https://doi.org/10.1002/hipo.23610)). The discussion of Navratilova et al. calls freely moving RSC fields "at least an order of magnitude larger" than CA1 fields, but the papers read in the sweep hold no numbers behind that statement [secondary].

### Fewer RSC cells are spatial in four of five paired studies, and field counts do not differ

In four of five paired studies fewer RSC cells than CA1 cells are spatial, with RSC/CA1 share ratios of 0.50 to 0.68 [derived]. Mao et al. found 13% against 26%, but their CA1 imaging pooled 2 AAV mice and 1 Thy1 mouse, so the labels do not match, and Thy1 RSC gave 56%, 1,416 of 2,544 neurons [full text] ([Mao et al. 2017](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5557927)). Navratilova et al. found 27.0 ± 11.3% against 39.7 ± 13.0% in the familiar room and 19.1 against 28.1% in the novel one [full text]. In rats, Smith et al. found 23.0% (84 of 366) against 38.3% (205 of 535), and Subramanian et al. found 45% against 85% of cells tuned to position in a model, in different rats from the same lab [full text]. Lenkey et al. are the exception, with 20.5% in RSC (665 of 3,244 cells, 5 mice) against 17.4% in CA1 (6,196 neurons, 7 mice), a ratio of 1.18, with Thy1 mice in both groups [full text; ratio derived]. Shares follow the mouse line and the rule. Thy1 GP4.3 mice gave 56% and 0.50 ± 0.02 place cells, against 13% with AAV, and Lenkey et al.'s 20.5% in Thy1 mice came from a stricter rule [full text] ([Mao et al. 2017](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5557927); [Mao et al. 2018](https://doi.org/10.1073/pnas.1803224115)). In rats, the RSC share rose with training on the plus maze from 8.9% to 28.0% [full text] ([Smith, Barredo & Mizumori 2012](https://doi.org/10.1002/hipo.20958)).

On the 1.5 m belt, 0.555, 0.336 and 0.106 of RSC place cells had one, two and three fields, a mean of 1.55, against 0.752, 0.223 and 0.020 in CA1, a mean of 1.26. Mao et al. report no difference, chi-square P = 0.21 [full text; means derived from figure-read bars]. Per metre that is 1.03 against 0.84 fields per place cell [derived]. Esteves et al.'s sham Thy1 mice on a familiar 1.5 m belt gave 0.79, 0.20 and 0.006, a mean of 1.21, or 0.81 per metre [figure-read, derived] ([Esteves et al. 2023](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.isci.2023.105970)). On the 3 to 5 m virtual tracks, about 81% of familiar place cells had one field and 19% two or more, about 1.2 fields per place cell, or 0.24 to 0.38 per metre [figure-read, derived]. Over all imaged cells, RSC had 0.093 fields per cell per metre against 0.146 in CA1, a ratio of 0.64. In a novel room the RSC value fell to 63% of the familiar one, p = 0.0067, against 55% in CA1 [full text, figure-read] ([Navratilova et al. 2026](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC13092332)). The section on fields per cell per grid period converts these counts to grid periods.

### The one retrosplenial distance curve matches CA1 at r = 0.5

Mao et al. give the only RSC kernel drawn against distance. Population vectors held only place cells, each scaled from 0 to 1, in 1.5 cm bins smoothed with a 4.5 cm Gaussian. Read from the vector paths of Fig. 3d, the RSC curve falls to 0.5 at 13.2 cm and crosses zero at 37.8 cm, 25% of the belt, which the Discussion rounds to "~35 cm". It stays near -0.2 from 70 to 120 cm, with a minimum of -0.23 near 93 cm, and then rises toward +0.6 at 150 cm because the belt is a loop. CA1 imaged the same way falls to 0.5 at 13.4 cm and crosses zero at 33 cm, with a minimum of -0.26 near 76 cm [full text, figure-read] ([Mao et al. 2017](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5557927)). The two regions match at r = 0.5, a ratio of 0.99, and RSC crosses zero about 14% later [derived]. The RSC full width at r = 0.5 is 0.67 of the mean field width and 0.77 of the median, against 0.72 and 0.85 for CA1 [derived]. The superficial dysgranular, deep and superficial granular subsets all reach 0.5 at 13.3 to 14.1 cm [full text, figure-read]. Local cues set part of the RSC width. In Thy1 mice, removing the belt's tactile cues moved the r = 0.5 point from 9.7 to 9.8 cm out to 12.2 to 12.3 cm, and the zero crossing from 23.3 to 23.8 cm out to 36.8 cm. These values come from matrices read by colour, so they carry about ±0.03 in r. The text says CA1 kept "the same rapid decrease in correlation with distance" without cues [full text, figure-read].

Fischer et al. built a landmark-centred kernel in virtual reality. They correlated population vectors from two halves of the trials, 367 task-engaged dysgranular neurons on short trials and 418 on long ones, so the peak reaches only 0.82 and 0.76. With the vector at a visual landmark as the reference, correlation falls to 0.5 at 27.9 cm before and 23.5 cm after the landmark on short trials, a full width of 51.4 cm, and at 26.0 and 22.5 cm on long trials, 48.5 cm. It crosses zero 93.5 cm before and 70.2 cm after the landmark on short trials [full text, figure-read] ([Fischer et al. 2020](https://doi.org/10.7554/eLife.51458)). The curve is narrowest at the landmark and broader elsewhere on the track. Because its peak is below 1, r = 0.5 lies at 61 to 66% of the peak rather than at half of it, so the heat maps leave it out, as they leave out Blair et al.'s cross-half curve [derived].

No rat RSC kernel has numbers. Alexander and Nitz report that rat RSC population matrices from odd and even trials repeat at quarter-route spacing on a plus track with repeated segments, which CA1 matrices do not, but the audit could not check the text behind this [preprint; unverified] ([Alexander & Nitz 2017](https://doi.org/10.1101/100537)). A rat cross-half matrix exists for a 5.1 m T-maze lap, but its figure was not obtained [full text] ([Miller, Mau & Smith 2019](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.cub.2019.05.034)). The sweep found no RSC kernel for a space longer than 2.8 m.

### Other retrosplenial cell types are counted mostly as shares

Head-direction tuning in rat RSC is 49.29 ± 1.28° wide, two circular SDs, against 43.65 ± 1.13° in postsubiculum, a ratio of 1.13 [full text] ([Lozano et al. 2017](https://doi.org/10.1177/2398212817721859)). Head-direction cells are 8 to 10% of rat RSC cells in three studies [abstract; full text] ([Chen et al. 1994](https://doi.org/10.1007/BF00243212); [Jacob et al. 2017](https://doi.org/10.1038/nn.4465); [Zhang, Grieves & Jeffery 2022](https://doi.org/10.1523/JNEUROSCI.0619-22.2022)) and 4.9% in a fourth [full text] ([Alexander et al. 2020](https://doi.org/10.1126/sciadv.aaz2322)). In mice they are 11.5% of units recorded with tetrodes [full text, derived] ([van der Goes et al. 2024](https://doi.org/10.7554/eLife.82952)) and 14.0% of imaged neurons [full text] ([Sit & Goard 2023](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41467-023-37704-5)). Egocentric boundary cells are 17.3% of rat RSC cells, 38.7% in dysgranular and 9.9% in granular RSC, and their preferred distances cluster near the animal [full text, figure-read] ([Alexander et al. 2020](https://doi.org/10.1126/sciadv.aaz2322)). Egocentric bearing cells are 40% [full text] ([LaChance & Hasselmo 2024](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41467-024-52315-4)). Border cells are 9.0% of 5,415 rat RSC cells, with preferred wall distances peaking at 5 cm, and 39.2% of them are also egocentrically tuned [full text] ([van Wijngaarden et al. 2020](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7609058)). In mouse granular RSC, 60% of 740 neurons were vertex cells in at least one polygon chamber, with vertex fields of about 80 cm², an equal-area diameter of 10.1 cm, and 27% were border cells [full text; diameter derived] ([Park et al. 2024](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41467-024-51391-w)). On a T-maze stem, 28.3% and 21% of rat RSC cells fired differently by route at asymptote [full text] ([Vedder et al. 2017](https://doi.org/10.1093/cercor/bhw192); [Miller, Mau & Smith 2019](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.cub.2019.05.034)), and 13.4% of rat RSC cells were tuned to distance from a goal [preprint] ([Chen, Wei, Tang & Xu 2026](https://doi.org/10.7554/eLife.112092.1)). In connected rooms, rat RSC maps repeated from room to room after rotation, while hippocampal cells remapped in 91 to 100% of cases [preprint] ([Laurent et al. 2026](https://doi.org/10.7554/eLife.109951.1)). In mice, 3.5% of RSC units coded the direction of a shelter relative to the animal [full text] ([Campagner et al. 2023](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41586-022-05553-9)).

Three gaps limit these comparisons. No rat RSC kernel has numbers, so the wider rat fields have no population counterpart. No study varies environment size and counts RSC fields. No study pairs anterior and posterior RSC place fields. Lenkey et al. imaged at AP -2.2 mm and Mao et al. near the caudal end, with different rules and mouse lines [full text].

## Non-grid MEC fields measure about 1.5 grid spacings in one mouse study and did not grow with grid scale

An audited sweep of non-grid cells in medial entorhinal cortex (MEC) found 32 field rows from 19 studies and 3 kernel rows from one study (sweep_mec_nongrid.md and audit_sweep_mec.md in the literature notes). Most papers describe these cells by their share of the population and by their stability or remapping. Only one study gives a non-grid field size, and it also gives grid spacing in the same mice.

### One study gives non-grid field size and grid spacing in the same mice

Mallory et al. recorded dorsal MEC in HCN1 knockout mice and their wild-type littermates, 527 MEC cells in 12 control mice, with grid cells 386 ± 222 µm from the dorsal border. In the controls, 32 "spatially stable" cells that were neither grid nor border cells had a mean field size of 2,473.45 ± 2,233.2 cm² (mean ± SD), an equal-area diameter of 56.1 cm. One SD either side of the mean area spans 17.5 to 77.4 cm. Their fields covered 43.24 ± 30.72% of the box [full text; diameters derived] ([Mallory et al. 2018](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41593-017-0055-3)). Grid spacing in the same mice was 36.26 ± 9.27 cm across 37 grid cells, so the non-grid diameter is 1.55 grid spacings [full text; ratio derived]. The 86 border cells had a border width of 14.43 ± 6.53 cm. Mallory et al. define it as the mean distance from the wall to the inner edge of each field, so it is a width perpendicular to the wall, 0.40 grid spacings [full text; ratio derived]. The paper's grid "field size", 489.29 ± 301.62 cm², is not a rate-map field. The Fig. 1 legend says it "was determined from the radius of the circle around the center field of the autocorrelation map". So it cannot be set against the non-grid areas, and this review gives no ratio of non-grid to grid field size [full text].

Four limits apply to the 1.55. It uses the mean of each cell's mean field area, and the SD is about as large as the mean, so a typical cell may have smaller fields. The arena size and the field rule are in the paywalled Online Methods, and a box caps the fields of broadly firing cells. Grid spacing comes from each cell's autocorrelogram without module labels, and a mean of 36 cm, with the smallest spacing per mouse at a median of 26.6 cm, points to the one or two most dorsal modules. The class is strict, 32 of 527 cells, 6.1% [full text; share derived].

The knockout gives a causal test inside dorsal MEC. Grid spacing rose 16%, from 36.26 to 42.16 ± 10.53 cm across 62 grid cells, p = 0.002. Non-grid field area did not change, 2,692.29 ± 2,768.8 against 2,473.45 cm², p = 0.90, n = 38. Neither did the share of the box the fields covered, 45.58 against 43.24%, p = 0.88, or border width, 14.23 against 14.43 cm, p = 0.76 [full text] ([Mallory et al. 2018](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41593-017-0055-3)). As a diameter the knockout non-grid field is 58.5 cm, 1.39 knockout grid spacings [derived]. In these recordings non-grid field size did not follow a change in grid scale.

### Non-grid cells have a third of the grid cells' field count

Hardcastle et al. counted fields in boxes that were mostly 1 m across: 100 × 100 cm in 228 of 275 sessions, 91.4 cm in 29 and 70 cm in 18. A field was a connected region of at least 5 bins above 1 Hz and above 20% of the peak rate. Non-grid cells that encoded position in a model had 1.90 ± 0.10 fields (mean ± SEM, n = 307), border cells 1.55 ± 0.23 (n = 42) and grid cells 5.69 ± 0.35 (n = 101). Non-grid cells therefore had a third of the grid count in the same boxes [full text; ratio derived] ([Hardcastle et al. 2017](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.neuron.2017.03.025)). The section on fields per cell per grid period gives these as 0.17 to 0.26 fields per λ² for non-grid cells [derived]. On cue-rich 8 m virtual tracks, a cue cell's fields covered about 0.4 of the cue regions and 0.2 of the rest under a lenient p < 0.3 field rule, and field number followed the number of cues [full text, derived] ([Kinkhabwala et al. 2020](https://doi.org/10.7554/eLife.43140)).

### The non-grid share depends on the criterion and the denominator

The share of MEC cells that are spatial but not grid runs from 6% to about 64%, and it depends on the criterion and on what the denominator counts [derived]. The table gives each study's counts and the audit's caveats on each denominator.

| Study | Animal, site | Non-grid spatial share | Grid share | Criterion and denominator | Status |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| [Diehl et al. 2017](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5444540/) | Rat, dorsal MEC, layers 2 and 3, 345 cells | 67.5% of spatial cells, about 64% of all cells | 17.7% of spatial cells | Shuffles of spatial information and split-half correlation; about 95% of cells were spatial, and the classes add to 94.5% of them | full text, derived |
| [Diehl et al. 2019](https://doi.org/10.1002/hipo.23017) | Rat, dorsal MEC, 313 cells | About 90% of cells spatial in 80 to 100 cm boxes and 55% in a 120 cm box, all classes together | Not separated | As Diehl 2017; 45 of the 313 cells, from 3 rats, were reported before, probably in Diehl 2017 | full text |
| [Hardcastle et al. 2017](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.neuron.2017.03.025) | Mouse, layers 2 and 3, 794 cells | 307 of 794, 38.7% | 101 of 794, 12.7% | Position coding in a model, not grid or border; all recorded cells | full text, derived |
| [Krupic et al. 2012](https://doi.org/10.1126/science.1222403) | Rat, dorsal MEC, 239 cells | 81 of 239 periodic non-grid cells, 34% | 75 of 239, 31% | Two-dimensional Fourier periodicity; MEC cells only, parasubiculum excluded | full text, derived |
| [Pérez-Escobar et al. 2016](https://doi.org/10.7554/eLife.16937) | Mouse, dorsal MEC and parasubiculum, 880 cells | 226 of 880, 25.7% | 139 of 880, 15.8% | Score thresholds; 12 of 49 recording sites lay at the border of MEC and parasubiculum or in parasubiculum, so the share is not MEC-only | full text, derived |
| [Low et al. 2021](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.neuron.2021.07.005) | Mouse, 4 m virtual track, 1,379 cells | 24.57 ± 3.12% spatially stable other cells | 18.26 ± 3.81% | Classes from gain sessions; session means | full text |
| [Tang et al. 2014](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.neuron.2014.11.009) | Rat, dorsal layer 2, 193 cells | About 30 of 193, 16% | 22 of 193, 11% | Spatial-information shuffle; assumes grid and border cells are subsets of the 63 spatial cells | full text, derived |
| [Tang et al. 2015](https://doi.org/10.1523/JNEUROSCI.0696-15.2015) | Rat, layer 3, 69 cells | 19 of 69, 28%, all irregular | None | Spatial information | abstract |
| [Mallory et al. 2018](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41593-017-0055-3) | Mouse, dorsal MEC, 527 cells | 32 of 527 spatially stable cells, 6.1% | 37 of 527, 7.0% | Stability shuffle at the 95th percentile, a strict class | full text, derived |
| [Miao et al. 2017](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.cell.2017.08.050) | Mouse, dorsal MEC, two cohorts | 214 aperiodic spatial cells | 248 grid cells | Spatial information above the 99th percentile; counts only | full text |

Named non-grid classes add more shares. Object-vector cells were 13.6% of cells, 67 of 492 in 7 mice [full text] ([Andersson, Moser & Moser 2021](https://doi.org/10.1038/s42003-021-02727-5)), and 17.7 ± 7.8% in animals chosen because they had more object-vector than grid cells [full text] ([Obenhaus et al. 2022](https://doi.org/10.1073/pnas.2121655119)). Cue cells were about 13% of 789 units, but 28% of them were also grid cells and 11% border cells. Non-grid, non-border cue cells are therefore about 8% of units if the two overlaps do not coincide [full text; 8% derived] ([Kinkhabwala et al. 2020](https://doi.org/10.7554/eLife.43140)). Border cells were 5.3% of cells (42 of 794) in one study and 7.2% (63 of 880) in another [full text, derived] ([Hardcastle et al. 2017](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.neuron.2017.03.025); [Pérez-Escobar et al. 2016](https://doi.org/10.7554/eLife.16937)), 9.3% of spatial cells in a third [full text] ([Diehl et al. 2017](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5444540/)), 14.1% (160 of 1,136) in a fourth [full text, derived] ([Campbell et al. 2018](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41593-018-0189-y)) and 13.23 ± 2.07% on a virtual track [full text] ([Low et al. 2021](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.neuron.2021.07.005)). In Neuropixels recordings from four rats in a 1.5 m box, 74 to 92% of units fell outside grid modules. That count includes non-spatial units and parasubiculum, and one rat's two recording days probably count some units twice [derived from source data] ([Gardner et al. 2022](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41586-021-04268-7)).

### Non-grid cells are less stable than grid cells and remap more

Between sessions in matching boxes, non-grid spatial cells had a median spatial correlation of 0.62 against 0.85 and 0.70 for grid cells, and median displacements of 4.4 and 4.7 cm against 2.4 and 2.6 cm [full text] ([Diehl et al. 2017](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5444540/)). Spatially periodic non-grid cells, pooled with parasubicular cells, correlated at r = 0.45 ± 0.02 between sessions on one day against 0.54 ± 0.02 for grid cells, and at 0.44 against 0.53 across days [full text] ([Krupic et al. 2012](https://doi.org/10.1126/science.1222403)). When the box changed shape or colour in the same room, non-grid fields moved a median 13.1 and 15.3 cm more than between matching boxes, while grid patterns moved 1.3 and 2.8 cm more. Across rooms the extra movement was 28.9 cm for non-grid cells and 20.9 cm for grid cells. Non-grid map correlations fell to 0.29 and 0.26 after the shape and colour changes, and grid correlations to 0.72 and 0.42 [full text] ([Diehl et al. 2017](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5444540/)). Between two 1.5 m arenas, 196 of 271 non-grid spatial cells in rats trained on a reward task remapped, with a mean correlation of 0.32 ± 0.22, against 0.41 ± 0.27 in foraging controls, where 49 of 100 remapped [full text] ([Butler, Hardcastle & Giocomo 2019](https://doi.org/10.1126/science.aav5297)). Between morning and afternoon sessions in one box, 16% of non-grid cells changed their spatial pattern and 16% their rate, while grid cells showed no systematic change. Cells whose spatial pattern changed were more common in layer 3, Chi-Sq = 5.88, p < 0.05, but cells whose rate changed were not [full text] ([Diehl et al. 2019](https://doi.org/10.1002/hipo.23017)). Of spatially periodic cells, 11% switched between grid and non-grid patterns within an environment and 32% between environments [full text] ([Krupic et al. 2012](https://doi.org/10.1126/science.1222403)). One within-session measure points the other way. On a virtual track, border cells had a half-session stability of 0.54 ± 0.02 against 0.40 ± 0.02 for grid cells [full text] ([Campbell et al. 2018](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41593-018-0189-y)).

### No study measures non-grid fields along the dorsoventral axis, and no non-grid kernel exists

No study measures non-grid field size at more than one dorsoventral level. Diehl et al. write that grid and non-grid cells "were highly intermixed along the dorso-ventral axis", but they give no sizes by level [full text] ([Diehl et al. 2017](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5444540/)). Hardcastle et al. found that "the degree of mixed selectivity did not change from dorsal to ventral MEC" over 0 to 1.54 mm, which concerns coding type, not field size [full text] ([Hardcastle et al. 2017](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.neuron.2017.03.025)). Savelli et al. recorded dorsal and ventral MEC, but only the abstract could be read [abstract] ([Savelli, Yoganarasimha & Knierim 2008](https://doi.org/10.1002/hipo.20511)). The knockout result is the only test of whether non-grid fields follow grid scale, and it stays inside dorsal MEC, so whether non-grid fields widen toward the ventral pole as grids do is unknown [inference]. No within-environment population kernel exists for non-grid MEC cells or for all MEC cells. Brun et al.'s kernels on the 18 m track come from grid cells only. They are 32, 47 and 67 cm wide at r = 0.5 at three levels, 0.16 to 0.19 of the median spacing, and "Repetitive bands were not observed" [full text, derived] ([Brun et al. 2008](https://doi.org/10.1002/hipo.20504)). The Neuropixels reanalysis in the second section used distance-tuned cells that the preprint treats as grid cells, and it gives no r = 0.5 width [preprint] ([Dasbehera, Dogra & Redman 2025](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.08292v2)). In the original recordings, 8 of 24 grid cells and 5 of 134 non-grid cells were distance tuned [full text] ([Campbell et al. 2021](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8437084)).

The sweep lists further gaps: non-grid field size in rats, where Diehl et al.'s Table S1 and Butler et al.'s Fig. S10A may hold it but could not be opened; non-grid fields in spaces larger than about 1.5 m; border field length along the wall; and object-vector field size. A boundary-vector criterion passed 20 of 860 MEC cells, 14 of them also border cells. Their fields sat 8.2 ± 0.7 cm from the wall, though the text does not say whether that mean covers the 6 remaining cells or all 20 [full text, supplement] ([Hoydal et al. 2019](https://doi.org/10.1038/s41586-019-1077-7)).

## A third to a half of CA1 fires in any one environment

In a standard small environment, roughly 20 to 50% of dorsal CA1 pyramidal cells have place fields and most of the rest are silent or nearly so. The estimate holds across methods but depends heavily on the denominator and the criterion. Thompson and Best isolated pyramidal cells under anaesthesia and then recorded them awake. **Nearly two thirds were silent in the test environment**, and over 92% of the spontaneously active pyramidal cells were place cells in at least one environment. Reanaesthesia and antidromic stimulation confirmed that the silent cells were still being recorded [abstract] ([Thompson & Best 1989](https://doi.org/10.1523/JNEUROSCI.09-07-02382.1989)). In novel and familiar environments, "less than half of the neurons in areas CA1 and CA3 are active whereas the rest are essentially silent", and CA1 fired about twice as many spikes in novel environments [abstract] ([Karlsson & Frank 2008](https://doi.org/10.1523/JNEUROSCI.4261-08.2008)). An early estimate was only an impression, that about 65% of well-isolated complex-spike units behaved as place cells, and its authors wrote that "a proper estimate will require additional work" [full text] ([Muller & Kubie 1987](https://doi.org/10.1523/JNEUROSCI.07-07-01951.1987)). In tracks under 5 m or arenas under 1 m², about 20 to 50% of dorsal CA1 pyramidal cells become place cells, according to the literature summary of Rich et al. [full text] ([Rich et al. 2014](https://www.its.caltech.edu/~jkenny/nb250c/papers/rich_14.pdf)). The criterion alone can double the answer. In the same open-field data, **74.7% of CA1 cells had fields under a rate-only criterion and 37.3% when spatial coherence was added**, and on a 250 cm track the figures were 64.2% and 48.0% for CA1 and 31.0% and 16.8% for CA3 [full text] ([Mizuseki et al. 2012](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3718552/)). A 2025 analysis says published estimates range from 20% to 90% and argues that in a familiar environment all active pyramidal cells are place cells [abstract] ([Talpir et al. 2025](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.isci.2025.112489)).

Other methods agree roughly. Arc expression marked about 40% of CA1 pyramidal cells after exploration [abstract] ([Chawla et al. 2005](https://doi.org/10.1002/hipo.20091)) and about 35% of CA1 against 18% of CA3 for one context [abstract] ([Vazdarjanova & Guzowski 2004](https://doi.org/10.1523/JNEUROSCI.0350-04.2004)). In mice imaged on an 84 cm track, 31 ± 1% of tracked cells were active per session, about 20% had a field in either direction and 12 ± 1% per direction, and each field was active on only 17 ± 14% of passes [full text] ([Ziv et al. 2013](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3784308)). Two-photon imaging on a virtual track found 15 to 20% place cells, 91% of them with one field [full text] ([Dombeck et al. 2010](https://doi.org/10.1038/nn.2648)). Of 9 classifiable whole-cell recordings in a novel arena, 4 cells had place fields and 5 were silent [full text] ([Epsztein, Brecht & Lee 2011](https://www.inmed.fr/wp-content/uploads/2014/04/11-epsztein_brecht_lee_2011.pdf)).

The regional ordering is consistent. Per environment, about 88% of hilar mossy cells, 29% of CA3 cells and **9% of granule cells** were active in rats, and 25 of 37 granule cells, 6 of 28 CA3 cells and 1 of 23 hilar cells were silent in all four rooms. Sleep rates were 0.10, 0.13 and 0.70 Hz for granule, CA3 and hilar cells. Cells had to fire in both baseline sleep sessions to be counted, so silent cells were included [full text] ([GoodSmith et al. 2017](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5300955/)). In rat granule cell layer recordings, 88% of cells fired below 0.5 Hz [abstract] ([Jung & McNaughton 1993](https://doi.org/10.1002/hipo.450030209)). Arc mapping across many environments suggests that "possibly 90-95%" of granule cells are effectively retired [abstract] ([Alme et al. 2010](https://doi.org/10.1002/hipo.20810)), and imaged mouse granule cells produced about one calcium event every 2 to 5 min [abstract] ([Pilz et al. 2016](https://doi.org/10.1523/JNEUROSCI.3065-15.2016)). CA2 had 68.2% of cells active per session against 59.2% in CA1 and 48.1% in CA3 [full text] ([Mankin et al. 2015](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4392894/)).

Recruitment grows with environment size without a clear ceiling, but slowly. On the 48 m track, with silent cells counted through flanking sleep sessions, **recruitment reached only about 65% of CA1 cells at 48 m**, 46 to 77% across rats. The fitted distribution matched the recruitment curve, KS = 0.05, P = 0.48, and its extrapolation reaches 90% at about 500 m [full text] ([Rich et al. 2014](https://www.its.caltech.edu/~jkenny/nb250c/papers/rich_14.pdf)). The fitted parameters predict 0.14^0.57 = 0.33 of cells with no field on 48 m, close to the observed 35% [derived]. Some cells silent on the track formed fields in a second novel environment [full text] ([Rich et al. 2014](https://www.its.caltech.edu/~jkenny/nb250c/papers/rich_14.pdf)). In a box 5.6 times the area of a cylinder, the active subset grew in every region, p < 0.001 [full text] ([Park, Dvorak & Fenton 2011](https://journals.plos.org/plosone/article?id=10.1371/journal.pone.0022349)). In bats, only 36 of 67 CA1 cells, 53.7%, were active in flight in a 6 m tunnel, while all 235 cells in the 200 m dataset were [full text] ([Eliav et al. 2021](https://www.weizmann.ac.il/brain-sciences/labs/ulanovsky/sites/brain-sciences.labs.ulanovsky/files/2024-11/Eliav2021.pdf)), and more than 80% of both CA1 and CA3 cells were place cells in the 200 m tunnel [full text] ([Maimon et al. 2026](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC13421320)). The bat denominators may exclude cells that never fired, which would make them larger than Rich's sleep-verified fractions [inference].

The share of cells active at any one location is small. Across four arenas up to 8.75 m², about **15% of place cells fired above 1 Hz at a given location**, with a mean rate of 0.52 Hz per place cell, and both values were constant across location and environment [full text] ([Tanni, de Cothi & Barry 2022](https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/PMC9616721/fullTextXML)). Multiplying place-cell fraction by field coverage gives about 3% of imaged CA1 cells per point and direction on the 84 cm track, 4 to 6% on the virtual track, and about 14% of CA1 against 3% of CA3 on the 250 cm track, ignoring multiple fields and directionality [derived]. Granule cells must be well under 1% [inference].

Silence belongs to a cell in an environment, not to the cell permanently. In whole-cell recordings, silent directions still received a subthreshold depolarization of 2.9 ± 0.3 mV against 12.8 ± 2.8 mV in place-field directions, place cells had lower spike thresholds, and future place cells fired more of their spikes in bursts in response to current steps before the animal entered the maze, 0.80 against 0.24 [full text] ([Epsztein, Brecht & Lee 2011](https://www.inmed.fr/wp-content/uploads/2014/04/11-epsztein_brecht_lee_2011.pdf)). A small uniform depolarization of a silent CA1 cell during behaviour made a place field appear, reversibly [abstract] ([Lee, Lin & Lee 2012](https://doi.org/10.1126/science.1221489)). Slow-wave-sleep rate before exposure predicted later field count, r = 0.45 [full text] ([Rich et al. 2014](https://www.its.caltech.edu/~jkenny/nb250c/papers/rich_14.pdf)), and the same neurons' log rates correlated between two mazes at r = 0.65 in CA1 and 0.59 in CA3 [full text] ([Mizuseki & Buzsáki 2013](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.celrep.2013.07.039)). Across 11 rooms, 342 CA3 cells were analysed, of which 210 were active in at least one room, 6% in six or more rooms and under 3% in eight or more, from search snippets of the paper and a reanalysis preprint [unverified; preprint] ([Alme et al. 2014](https://doi.org/10.1073/pnas.1421056111); [eleven-room reanalysis](https://www.biorxiv.org/content/10.1101/2024.11.15.623794v3.full)). If each cell activated independently in each room at the mean rate, about 80% would be active somewhere and 0.15% in six or more rooms, so the observed counts show both more never-active cells and more multi-room cells than independence predicts [derived from unverified counts]. Within one familiar environment imaged over 45 days, 57 ± 1% of cells ever active were active in only one or two of ten sessions, 2.8% in all ten, and the place-cell sets of two sessions overlapped by about 25% at 5 days and 15% at 30 days [full text] ([Ziv et al. 2013](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3784308)).
